--- Page 9 ---
# PREFACE.

THE foundation of this work will be found in *The Lives of Alchemystical Philosophers; with a Critical Catalogue of Books in Occult Chemistry, and a Selection of the most celebrated Treatises on the Theory and Practice of the Hermetic Art*, <note>The "Hermetic Art" refers to alchemy, named after Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary founder of the tradition.</note> which was published in the year 1815 by Lackington, Allen, & Company, of Finsbury Square, London. This anonymous book has been attributed by certain collectors to Francis Barrett, author of the notorious treatise entitled *The Magus, or Celestial Intelligencer*; but it may be safely affirmed that, in both its subject matter and its treatment, it far transcends the extremely meager capacities of that credulous amateur in occultism. It is indeed a work of much sense and unpretentious discrimination, and is now a bibliographical rarity which is highly prized by its possessors.

The independent researches which have supplemented the biographical materials of the original compilation have produced in the present volume what is practically a new work under an old title. Those lives which have been left substantially untouched regarding facts have been more or less rewritten with a view to compressing wordy passages <note>original: "prolixities"</note> and eliminating archaic forms, which would be inconsistent in a work so extensively modified by the addition of new details. The "Alphabetical Catalogue of Works...

<summary>This preface introduces a modernized and expanded edition of a 1815 biographical dictionary of alchemists, distancing the original work from the occultist Francis Barrett.</summary>
<keywords>Alchemy, Alchemystical Philosophers, Francis Barrett, The Magus, Hermetic Art, bibliography, occult history</keywords>

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>This page concludes the author's preface, detailing the sources used for the biographical section and providing a critical correction regarding the identity of the alchemist Eirenaeus Philalethes.</meta>

<page-num>6</page-num>
->PREFACE.<-

The section on "Hermetic Philosophy" <note>The tradition of alchemy and esoteric wisdom attributed to Hermes Trismegistus.</note> has been considerably enlarged from sources such as Langlet du Fresnoy’s *History of Hermetic Philosophy* <note>original: "Histoire de la Philosophie Hermétique"</note>. The preliminary account of the "Physical Theory and Practice of the Great Work" <note>original: "Magnum Opus"; the process of creating the Philosopher's Stone.</note> is a brief original sketch which will provide readers unacquainted with alchemy some idea of the processes used by recognized experts. The introductory essay on the goal of alchemical philosophy advocates for new and important views regarding the great question of "psychal chemistry" <note>The theory that alchemical processes refer to spiritual or psychological transformation rather than just physical base metals.</note>, and evaluates the true worth of the conflicting theories proposed by various schools of Hermetic interpretation.

### IMPORTANT NOTE.

I am forced to add to this Preface a correction of one or two errors of vital importance which were unfortunately overlooked in the text. On page 188, line 18, the date was intended to read 1643. On page 189, line 5, read *in his thirty-third year* <note>original: "anno trigesimo tertio"</note> instead of *in his thirtieth year*; and on line 6, read *in his twenty-third year* <note>original: "anno vigesimo tertio"</note> instead of *in his twentieth year*. 

However, even if these corrections restore the passage to its original state, a discovery I made while this work was being printed has entirely cancelled its value. I have been privileged to see the original edition of Philalethes’ *The Open Entrance* <note>original: "Introitus Apertus"</note>—a small octavo pamphlet in its original paper cover, as it was published at Amsterdam in the year 1667. This edition definitely establishes that its mysterious author was born in or about the year 1623—two years later than the Welsh adept Thomas Vaughan, with whom he has long been identified. This original edition is excessively scarce; I believe I am the only English mystic who has seen it during the present generation. The reader must please understand that the calculation in the pages referred to was based on the date 1643; in light of the original edition, this date proved erroneous. By a curious chance, the date that was accidentally printed turns out to be correct, though it invalidates the original calculation.

<summary>The author identifies his primary sources and provides a significant historical correction regarding the birth date of the alchemist Philalethes, distinguishing him from Thomas Vaughan.</summary>
<keywords>Hermetic Philosophy, Langlet du Fresnoy, Magnum Opus, psychal chemistry, Eirenaeus Philalethes, Thomas Vaughan, Introitus Apertus, alchemy history</keywords>

# INTRODUCTORY ESSAY

## ON THE TRUE PRINCIPLES AND NATURE OF THE GREAT WORK, AND ON ITS RELATION TO SPIRITUAL CHEMISTRY. <note>original: "MAGNUM OPUS"; the final goal of the alchemical process, often equated with the creation of the Philosopher's Stone.</note>

Those who are unfamiliar with modern alchemical criticism—even if they have some knowledge of the mystical labyrinth of the *Assembly of the Philosophers* <note>original: "*turba philosophorum*"; one of the oldest Latin alchemical texts, written as a transcript of a fictional meeting of ancient sages.</note>—will probably be astonished to learn that the opinions of experts are divided. This division concerns not only the methods of the mysterious Hermetic science <note>A philosophical and spiritual tradition based on writings attributed to the legendary Hermes Trismegistus.</note>, but the very objective of alchemy itself. While it is accepted that alchemy is concerned with transmutation <note>The process of changing one substance or state into another.</note>, a select group of respected students of occultism strongly denies that it aims for the transmutation of metals, or any physical substance, into material gold. The transcendental theory of alchemy which they propose is steadily gaining favor, although the two textbooks that currently represent this view are both out of print and both extremely rare.

In the year 1850, a book titled *A Suggestive Inquiry concerning the Hermetic Mystery and Alchemy, being an attempt to recover the Ancient Experiment of Nature*, was published anonymously in London by a woman of great intellectual talent <note>Mary Anne Atwood (1817–1910). She and her father later famously attempted to buy back and burn every copy of the book, fearing they had revealed secrets that should remain hidden.</note>. However, it was withdrawn from circulation almost immediately for reasons unknown, which has since given rise to several baseless rumors. This curious and valuable volume, written in the quaint style of the—

<summary>This page introduces the debate between physical and spiritual interpretations of alchemy, highlighting the "transcendental theory" which suggests alchemy is about inner transformation rather than material gold. # LIVES OF ALCHEMISTICAL PHILOSOPHERS.

In the last century, the views in question originated and opened the controversy.

Fifteen years after the appearance of the *Suggestive Inquiry* <note>Published in 1850 by Mary Anne Atwood; it argued that alchemy was a method of spiritual enlightenment.</note>, an American writer named Hitchcock—after arriving at parallel conclusions through apparently independent research—published his findings anonymously in 1865. His work, *Remarks on Alchemy and the Alchemists*, was a small octavo volume of very considerable interest. While the previous author placed a psychic interpretation on the secrets of Hermetic symbolism <note>original: "arcana of Hermetic typology"</note>, Mr. Hitchcock adopted a moral interpretation. This brought the general subject within the reach of most ordinary readers and attracted considerable attention as a result.

The views expressed in these works have filtered slowly through the culture. Combined with the Paracelsian theory of the "psychic manufacture" of material gold through the agency of the "inner magnet" <note>original: "interior magnes"; a concept in Paracelsian thought referring to a spiritual force within man that can attract and transform energies</note>, they have considerably influenced the revived occultism of the present day. This question, even when viewed from its most basic level, is one of the most curious to be found within the entire circle of esoteric archaeology. For students whose interest in the great alchemical mystery is more than just historical, it is truly of primary <note>original: "palmary"</note> interest and supreme importance. In an account of the lives and labors of the Hermetic adepts, this theory calls for adequate consideration. After careful research, I believe I have discovered a true alchemical theory that will be equally acceptable to all schools of interpretation.

The supreme and avowed object of every high priest of the mysteries <note>original: "hierophant"</note>, as well as every candidate and pretender, in the <term>Great Art</term> <note>original: "ars magna"</note> discovered by Hermes Trismegistus, has been commonly supposed to be the chemical manufacture of material gold from commercially inferior substances. On the other hand, Hitchcock, by organizing an impressive series of word-for-word...

<page-num>11</page-num>

->INTRODUCTORY ESSAY.<-

...citations from writers of all ages and all nationalities, he undertakes to demonstrate that the concealed subject of every true <term>adept</term> <note>An "adept" is a person who has mastered the secrets of alchemy or occult science.</note> is one only—namely, MAN, who is <term>triune</term> <note>Composed of three parts, typically body, soul, and spirit in this context.</note>, and that “the object also is one, namely, his improvement. The method itself is no less one: nature guided by art in the school of nature, and acting in harmony with it; for the art is nothing but ‘nature acting through man.’” Again, “the genuine <term>alchemists</term> were not in pursuit of worldly wealth or honors. Their real object was the perfection, or, at least, the improvement of man. According to this theory, such perfection lies in a certain unity, a living sense of the unity of the human with the divine nature. The attainment of this I can compare to nothing so well as to the experience known in religion as the NEW BIRTH. The desired perfection, or unity, is a state of the soul, *a condition of Being*, and not a mere condition of KNOWING. This condition of Being is a development of the nature of man from within, the result of a process by which whatever is evil in our nature is cast out or suppressed—under the name of <term>superfluities</term> <note>In alchemy, "superfluities" are the useless or impure parts of a substance that must be removed.</note>—and the good is thereby allowed opportunities for free activity. As this result is scarcely accessible to the unassisted natural man, and requires the cooperation of divine power, it is called the *<term>Gift of God</term>* <note>original: "Donum Dei"</note>.”

When the individual man, by a natural and appropriate process, devoid of haste or violence, is brought into unity with himself by the harmonious action of intelligence and will, he is on the threshold of comprehending that transcendent Unity which is the perfection of the totality of Nature, “for what is called the ‘absolute,’ the ‘absolute perfection,’ and the perfection of Nature, are one and the same.”

In the symbolism of the <term>alchemists</term>, this writer tells us that *<term>sulphur</term>* <note>In spiritual alchemy, sulphur often represents the active, solar, or passionate principle of the soul.</note> signifies Nature, and *<term>mercury</term>* <note>Mercury often represents the passive, lunar, or intellectual principle.</note> the supernatural. The inseparable connection of the two in man is called *<term>the Sun</term>* <note>original: "Sol"; the balanced or perfected state of the soul.</note>,

<summary>This page describes Hitchcock’s theory that alchemy is a symbolic language for human self-improvement and spiritual rebirth, equating the alchemical process with the religious experience of becoming one with the divine.</summary>
<keywords>triune man, spiritual alchemy, New Birth, Donum Dei, Sulphur and Mercury, Sol, human perfection, E.A. Hitchcock</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the analysis of E.A. Hitchcock's 19th-century interpretation of alchemy, specifically focusing on his argument that the "Great Work" is a psychological and spiritual transformation of the human being, with the conscience acting as the primary tool.</meta>

<page-num>12</page-num>
# LIVES OF ALCHEMICAL PHILOSOPHERS.

but "as these three are seen to be indissolubly one, the terms may be used interchangeably." According to Hitchcock, the mystical and mysterious instrument of preparation in the work of alchemy is the conscience, which is called by a thousand misleading and admittedly inconsistent names. By means of this instrument, sparked into vital activity by a sense of the presence of God, the "matter of the stone"—namely, Man—is first purged and purified to make the internal realization of Truth possible. "By a figure of speech <note>original: "metonymy"</note>, the conscience itself is said to be purified, though, in fact, the conscience needs no purification, but only the man, so that the conscience may operate freely." *

One of the names given by the alchemists to the conscience, according to this theory, is that of a "middle substance" which partakes of an "azurine sulphur"—that is, of a celestial spirit—the Spirit of God. "The still small voice is in alchemy, as in Scripture, compared to a *fire*, which prepares the way for what many of the writers speak of as a *Light*."

Hitchcock elsewhere more emphatically asserts that there is only one subject within the wide circle of human interests that can provide an interpretation of the citations he gives, and it is that which is known by the theological name of spiritual <term>Regeneration</term> <note>In this context, regeneration refers to the spiritual rebirth or "New Birth" of a person.</note>. The alchemists investigated this gift of God as a work of Nature within Nature. "The repentance which in religion is said to begin conversion, is the 'philosophical contrition' of <term>Hermetic allegory</term> <note>Symbolic stories and teachings attributed to Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary founder of alchemy.</note>. It is the first step of man toward the discovery of his whole being. They also called it the black state of the matter, in which the work of dissolution was carried out,

---

* There is no need to assume a figure of speech <note>original: "metonymy"</note>. The conscience is a guide that education easily distorts. Therefore, assuming it is truly the instrument of the alchemists, it may very well need purification; except in the most general matters, it is at best an uncertain guide.

# INTRODUCTORY ESSAY.

calcination <note>original: "calcination"; the process of reducing a substance to powder through heat, symbolizing the purification of the soul</note>, separation, and so forth, after which results purification—the white state, which contains the red, just as the black state once contained the white. The evolution of the glorious and radiant red state resulted in the "fixation" or perfection of the matter; at that point, the soul was supposed to have entered into its true rest in God.

As this interpretation is concerned chiefly with the conscience, I have called it the moral theory of alchemy. However, Hitchcock, as a man of spiritual insight, could not fail to perceive that his explanatory method treated only of the "way." The formless light of an "End"—which he could not or would not discuss—is, by his own admission, continually glimmering before him.

For the rest, when the alchemists speak of a long life as one of the gifts of the Stone <note>The Philosopher's Stone</note>, he considers that they mean immortality. When they attribute to it the miraculous properties of a universal medicine, it is their intention to deny that evil has any positive qualities and, by inference, any permanence. When they assert that possessing the Stone results in the annihilation of covetousness and every illicit desire, they mean that all evil affections disappear before the light of the unveiled Truth. By the "transmutation of metals," they signified the conversion of man from a lower to a higher order of existence—from a natural life to a spiritual life—although even these expressions are inadequate to convey the real meaning of the adepts <note>original: "adepts"; those fully initiated into the secrets of alchemy</note>. The powers of an ever-active nature must be understood by such expressions as "fires," "menstruums" <note>original: "menstruums"; solvents used in alchemical processes</note>, and so on; these work in unison because they work within Nature, as the alchemists unanimously denied the existence of any disorder in God's creation.

In conclusion, Hitchcock states once more that his object is to point out the true subject of alchemy. He does not attempt to make its practical treatment plain to the *end* of

the divine operation <note>original: "sublime operation"</note>. It is, therefore, evident that he, at any rate, suspected the existence of more transcendent secrets which he felt unable to discuss and declined to speak of insufficiently.

The author of the "Suggestive Inquiry" <note>Mary Anne Atwood, who published *A Suggestive Inquiry into the Hermetic Mystery* in 1850</note> had already taken the higher standpoint of psychic interpretation and developed her remarkable principles, which I must attempt to reproduce as briefly as possible.

According to this work, the modern art of chemistry has no connection with alchemy except in its terminology, which was used by the masters <note>original: "adepts"</note> to hide their divine mysteries. The process of the entire Hermetic work is described with at least comparative clarity in the writings of the philosophers—with the exception of the *vessel*, which is a holy secret <note>original: "arcanum"</note>; without the knowledge of it, no one can attain the mastery <note>original: "magistery"</note>. Now, the publication of the writings of Jacob Böhme <note>A famous German Christian mystic (1575–1624) whose works influenced later alchemical thought</note> caused the alchemists of his time to fear that their art could no longer remain a secret, and that the mystic vase in particular would soon be revealed to everyone. This vase is the *chosen vessel of election* <note>original: *vas insigne electionis*; a Latin phrase often applied to St. Paul, referring here to the human being as a container of divine grace</note>—namely, MAN—who is the only subject that contains everything, and who alone needs to be investigated for the eventual discovery of all things. Modern masters <note>original: "adepts"</note> describe the life of man as a pure, naked, and unmixed fire of unlimited capability. Man, therefore, is the true laboratory of the Hermetic art; his life is the subject, the great distillery, the thing being distilled, and the result of the distillation; and self-knowledge is at the root of all alchemical tradition.

> "Modern discoveries are now tending to identify light—the common sustainer of life—as being in rhythmic agreement throughout the human circulatory system with the planetary spheres and the harmonious arrangements of the hidden medium in space; and as human physiology advances with..."

In addition to the faculties which he commonly exerts to communicate with the material universe, man possesses within him the germ of a higher faculty. The revelation and evolution of this faculty give intuitive knowledge of the hidden springs of nature. This <term>Wisdom-faculty</term> <note>The author refers to a latent spiritual or "gnostic" sense</note> operates in a magical manner and constitutes an alliance with <term>Omniscient Nature</term> <note>All-knowing Nature</note>, so that the illuminated understanding of its possessor perceives the structure of the universe and enjoys free clarity of thought in universal consciousness.

In support of this statement, it is argued that the evidence of natural reason—even in the affairs of common life—is intuition; that intuitive faith has a certainty above and independent of reason; and that the existence of universal truths in the human mind includes a promise far beyond itself. This is stable proof of another state of being, however much it remains unknown to our ordinary consciousness.

The true methods and conditions of self-knowledge are to be learned from the ancient writers. The discovery of the veritable <term>Light of alchemy</term> <note>The spiritual illumination or "Philosopher's Stone" of the mind</note> is the reward of an adequate scrutiny of true psychical experience. Alchemy proposes:

> “such a <term>reducation</term> <note>original: "reducation"; a leading back or restoration to a former state</note> of nature as shall discover this <term>latex</term> <note>original: "latex"; here meaning a hidden primordial fluid or essence</note> without destroying her vehicle, but only the modal life; and professes that this has not alone been proved possible, but that man, by rationally conditioning himself, has succeeded in developing into action the <term>Recreative Force</term>.”

The "One Thing" needful—the sole act which must be perfectly accomplished so that man may know himself—is the exaltation of the recognizing faculty into <term>intellectual reminiscence</term> <note>The Platonic idea that true knowledge is a form of remembering divine truths</note> by the adequately purified spirit. # LIVES OF ALCHEMISTICAL PHILOSOPHERS.

The transcendental philosophy of the mysteries entirely hinges on the purification of the whole understanding, without which they promise nothing.

The end in view is identical for Hermetists <note>Followers of the mystical traditions attributed to Hermes Trismegistus</note>, Theurgists <note>Those who practice a form of magic intended to invoke the presence of deities</note>, and the ancient Greek mysteries alike. It is the conscious and hypostatic union <note>A term from theology referring to a substantial, fundamental union of distinct natures</note> of the intellectual soul with Deity, and its participation in the life of God. However, the conception included in this divine name is one that is infinitely transcendental; and in Hermetic operations, above all, it must ever be remembered that God is within us. “The initiated person sees the Divine Light itself, without any form or figure—that light which is the true *star of the sun* <note>original: "astrum solis"</note>, the mineral spiritual sun, which is the Perpetual Motion of the Wise, and that Saturnian Salt <note>In alchemy, a reference to the "lead" or base matter of the soul being transformed</note>, which, when developed into intellect and made upright, subdues all nature to His will. It is the Midnight Sun of Apuleius, the Ignited Stone of Anaxagoras, the Triumphal Chariot of Antimony, the Armed Magnet of Helvetius, the Fiery Chariot of Mercaba <note>A reference to the "Merkabah" or celestial chariot in Jewish mysticism</note>, and the Stone with the new name written on it which is promised to him that overcomes, by the initiating Saviour of mankind.”

This method of interpreting Hermetic allegories is calculated to exalt the alchemists indefinitely in the estimation of all thinking minds. From being possibly greedy investigators of a side-path of physical science, they are transformed into dreamers of the sublimest imaginable dream; while if that which they conceived was actually accomplished, they are divine and illuminated monarchs who are enthroned on the pinnacles of eternity, having dominion over their infinite souls.

A theory so attractive, devised in the interests of men whom romance has already magnified in the golden <note>original: "auriferous"</note> cloud of mystery which envelopes both their claims and their persons, is eminently liable to be accepted on in—

Both Hitchcock <note>Ethan Allen Hitchcock (1798–1870), an American general who argued that alchemy was a symbolic mask for spiritual philosophy.</note> and the unparalleled woman to whom we are indebted for the *Suggestive Inquiry* <note>Mary Anne Atwood (1817–1910), author of "A Suggestive Inquiry into the Hermetic Mystery," a foundational text of the spiritual alchemy movement.</note> appeal to alchemical writings in support of their statements. A few of their quotations and commentaries must therefore be submitted to the reader.

The first point which strikes the alchemical student is the unanimous conviction of all the philosophers that certain initiatory exercises of a moral and spiritual kind are an indispensable preliminary to operations which are commonly supposed to be physical. Here the incongruity is evident, and it is therefore urged that the process itself is spiritual, and that it was materialized in the writings of the adepts to confuse and mislead the profane, as well as for the protection of esoteric psychologists in the days of the Inquisition and the stake.

The following preparation for the study of <term>Antimony</term> <note>In spiritual alchemy, antimony often symbolized the raw, chaotic state of the soul before purification.</note> is recommended by Basil Valentine <note>A legendary 15th-century alchemist, though the works attributed to him likely date to the late 16th century.</note>. “First, Invocation to God, with a certain heavenly intention, drawn from the bottom of a sincere heart and conscience, pure from all ambition, hypocrisy, and all other vices which have any affinity with these; such as arrogance, boldness, pride, luxury, petulance, oppression of the poor, and other similar evils, all of which are to be eradicated from the heart. Thus, when a man desires to prostrate himself before the throne of grace to obtain health, he may do so with a conscience free from unprofitable weeds, so that his body may be transmuted into a holy temple of God and be purged from all uncleanness. For God will not be mocked (of which I would earnestly admonish all), as worldly men, pleasing and flattering themselves with their own wisdom, think. God, I say, will not be mocked, but the Creator of all

...things will be invoked with reverential fear, and acknowledged with due obedience. . . This is so very true that I am certainly assured no impious person shall ever be a partaker of the <term>true medicine</term> <note>In spiritual alchemy, the "true medicine" represents the Elixir or Philosopher's Stone, viewed here as a spiritual cure for the soul's imperfections.</note>, much less of the eternal, <term>heavenly bread</term> <note>A reference to divine wisdom or spiritual sustenance.</note>. The second qualification is <term>contemplation</term> <note>Deep, focused meditation or observation.</note>, by which Basil says, "I understand an accurate attention to the business itself, under which these first considerations will be noted: what are the circumstances of anything; what is the matter; what is the form; from where do its operations proceed; from where is it infused and implanted; and how is it generated. . . also how the body of everything may be. . . resolved into its <term>first matter</term> <note>original: "materia prima"; the primitive, formless base of all substance that alchemists sought to isolate.</note> or essence. One is called possible, the other impossible. The latter consists of copious <term>cogitations</term> <note>Abundant or vast thoughts.</note> which never proceed to effects, nor exhibit any form of matter which can be felt, as if any should endeavor to comprehend the eternity of the Most High, which is vain and impossible; indeed, it is a sin against the Holy Ghost to so arrogantly pry into the Divinity itself, which is immense, infinite, and eternal, and to subject the incomprehensible counsel of the secrets of God to human inquiry. The other part of <term>contemplation</term> which is possible is called <term>theory</term>. This contemplates that which is perceived by touch and sight, and has a nature formed in time; this considers how that..."

# INTRODUCTORY ESSAY.

Nature may be helped and perfected by its own resolution; how every body may release from itself the good or evil, the venom or the medicine, latent within it; how destruction and composition are to be handled, so that, through a correct process without sophisticated deceits, the pure may be severed and separated from the impure. This separation is performed and established by various manual operations... some of which are commonly known by experience, while others are far removed from common experience. These are <term>calcination</term> <note>reducing a substance to powder through heat</note>, <term>sublimation</term> <note>heating a solid until it becomes a gas and then solidifies again</note>, <term>reverberation</term> <note>applying heat reflected from the roof of a furnace</note>, <term>circulation</term> <note>continuous distillation in a closed vessel</note>, <term>putrefaction</term> <note>decomposition through gentle heat</note>, <term>digestion</term> <note>prolonged exposure to gentle heat</note>, <term>distillation</term>, <term>cohobation</term> <note>repeated distillation of a liquid from the same solid matter</note>, <term>fixation</term> <note>making a volatile substance stable under heat</note>, and others like these. All the stages of these processes are found, learned, perceived, and revealed through the act of operating. 

From this, it will clearly appear what is movable, what is fixed, and what is white, red, black, blue, or green—namely, when the operation is correctly established by the craftsman. For it is possible that the operator may err and turn aside from the right way; but it is impossible for Nature to err when she is handled correctly. Therefore, if you should err so that nature cannot be entirely freed and released from the body in which it is held captive, return again to your path. Learn the theory more perfectly and inquire more practically into your method of operating, so that you may discover the foundation and certainty in the separation of all things, which is a matter of great concern. And this is the second foundation of philosophy which follows prayer; for the heart of the matter lies there, and is contained in these words: "Seek first the kingdom of God and his righteousness through prayer, and all other things shall be added unto you." <note>A reference to Matthew 6:33.</note>

The following passage states:

> “The light of this knowledge is the gift of God, which by His generosity He bestows upon whom He pleases. Let no one, therefore, set themselves to the study of this until, having cleared and purified their heart, they devote themselves wholly unto God and are emptied of all affection for impure things. Those who hold public honors and offices, or are always busy with private and necessary occupations, should not strive to attain the heights of this philosophy; for it requires the whole person. Once found, it possesses the seeker; and being possessed, it claims them from all long and serious employments, regarding all other things as foreign and of no value. Let the person who desires this knowledge clear their mind of all evil impulses, especially pride, which is an abomination to heaven and the gate of hell. Let them be frequent in prayer and charitable; have little to do with the world; abstain from too much socializing, and enjoy constant tranquility, so that the mind may be able to reason more freely in private and be more highly lifted up. For unless the mind is kindled with a beam of divine light, it will hardly be able to penetrate the hidden mysteries of truth. . . . A studious <term>Tyro</term> <note>original: "Tyro"; a beginner or novice</note> of a quick wit and constant mind, inflamed with the love of philosophy, very skilled in <term>natural philosophy</term> <note>original: "natural philosophy"; the study of nature and the physical universe, the precursor to modern science</note>, of a pure heart, perfect in manner, and mightily devoted to God—even if ignorant of chemistry—may enter with confidence the highway of Nature and read the books of the best philosophers. Let them seek out a clever companion and not despair of accomplishing their desire.”

Here Hitchcock <note>Ethan Allen Hitchcock (1798–1870), an American general and author who argued that alchemy was a spiritual rather than a physical pursuit</note> points out that the operation is obviously not chemical, for the chief instrument is determined and concentrated thinking on the loftiest intellectual planes. <meta>This page continues the introductory discussion on the spiritual and intellectual requirements for practicing alchemy, specifically addressing the necessity of divine favor over mere technical knowledge.</meta>

# INTRODUCTORY ESSAY.

Ignorance of chemistry is not necessarily a source of failure. In this connection, it must be remembered that the distinction between alchemy and chemistry can scarcely be said to have existed at the period of Jean d'Espagnet <note>Jean d'Espagnet (1564–1637) was a French politician and alchemist known for his work *Enchiridion Physicae Restitutae*.</note>, and the statement would at first sight seem almost equivalent to asserting that it was unnecessary to be versed in the properties of metals to accomplish the **great work** <note>original: *magnum opus*; the ultimate goal of alchemy, typically the creation of the Philosopher's Stone.</note>.

"Let a lover of truth," continues the author of the *Canons* <note>Referring to Espagnet's *Arcanum Hermeticae Philosophiae Opus*, often called the *Hermetic Canons*.</note>, "make use of but a few philosophers, but those of the best reputation and proven truth; let him suspect things that are quickly understood, especially regarding mystical names and secret operations, for truth lies hidden in obscurity. Philosophers never write more deceitfully than when they seem plain, nor ever more truly than when they write obscurely."

In the same manner, *The New Light of Alchemy*, falsely ascribed to Michael Sendivogius <note>Sendivogius (1566–1636) was a Polish alchemist; the work is now often attributed to Alexander Seton.</note>, and which is held in high regard among Hermetic <term>Hermetic: relating to the mystical and alchemical tradition following Hermes Trismegistus</term> students, declares that "the most commendable art of alchemy is the gift of God, and truly it is not to be attained except by the favor of God alone enlightening the understanding, together with a patient and devout humility, or by a visual demonstration from some experienced master."

In *The Hidden Magical Soul* <note>original: *Anima Magica Abscondita* (1650)</note>, Eugenius Philalethes <note>The pseudonym of Thomas Vaughan (1621–1666), a Welsh clergyman and alchemist.</note> gives the following advice to the student, whether of magic or alchemy: "Attempt nothing rashly. Prepare yourself until you are in harmony with Him whom you would host. You have three parts that are to receive, and there are three that give. Prepare your house for your God as best you can, and where you cannot, He will help you. When you have set your house in order, do not think your guest will come without invitation. You must weary Him with pious persistence. This is the way in which you must walk, in which you shall perceive a sudden enlightenment, **and it shall be in you with the Light of Fire, and with Fire** <note>original: *eritque in te cum Lumine Ignis, cum Igne*</note>."

"Wind, and with Wind there is Power; with Power there is Knowledge; and with Knowledge comes the integrity of a sound mind." <note>original: *Ventus, cum Vento Potestas, cum Potestate Scientia, cum Scientiâ sanæ mentis integritas.*</note> This is the chain of qualities that qualifies a magician. This is the place—namely, the abode of the <term>Archetype</term> <note>the original model or spiritual realm from which the physical world is derived</note>—where, if you can but once ascend and then descend—

> "Then it will be possible to travel often to the Archetypal World and return,
> and to behold the Father of all things"— <note>original: *Tunc ire ad Mundum Archetypum sæpe atque redire, Cunctarumque Patrem rerum spectare licebit*</note>

you have gained that spirit which knows how to distinguish and accomplish everything that extraordinary mathematicians, marvelous magicians, the envious alchemists who persecute nature, and the evil necromancers—who are worse than demons—dare to promise. He knows how to discern and effect these things himself, and does so without any crime, without offending God, and without injury to religion. <note>original: *Qui quicquid portentosi Mathematici, quicquid prodigiosi Magi, quicquid invidentes Naturæ persecutores Alchymistæ, quicquid Dæmonibus deteriores malefici Necromantes promittere audent. Ipse novit discernere et efficere idque sine omni crimine, sine Dei offensâ, sine Religionis injuriâ.*</note> Such is the power he shall receive who ascends from the noisy chaos of this world to the supernatural "still voice"—rising from this lowly earth and the mind to which his body is allied, to the spiritual, invisible elements of his soul."

After the same fashion, the even greater Eirenæus Philalethes <note>a famous 17th-century alchemist, now widely identified as the American George Starkey</note> declares that God alone communicates the whole secret of the <term>philosophers' water</term> <note>original: *aqua philosophorum*; the essential solvent or "mercury" used to create the Philosopher's Stone</note>, and that all those untaught by Him must wander in mists and error; however, it is revealed to those who labor in study and prayer.

Such quotations might be continued indefinitely. *The Concentrated Center of Nature* <note>original: *Centrum Naturæ Concentratum*</note>, attributed to Alipili and a treatise of some reputation, declares that "The highest wisdom consists in this: for man to know himself, because in him God has placed His eternal word (by which all things were made and upheld) to be his Light and Life, by which he is capable of knowing all things in time and eternity. . . . Therefore, let the high inquirers and searchers into the deep mysteries of nature learn first what they have in themselves before they seek in foreign matters outside of them; and by the divine power within them, let them first heal themselves and <term>transmute</term> <note>to change from one form or state into another, specifically the purification of the soul or the changing of base metals into gold</note> their own souls; then they may..."

### LIVES OF ALCHEMICAL PHILOSOPHERS.

> "...are locked up in the abyss of nature to those who devoutly worship him. And because no one is permitted to touch the mysteries of nature with unclean fingers, it is therefore necessary for all who attempt such matters to lay aside their natural blindness. From this blindness they may be freed by the light of the Holy Scripture and a steadfast faith; this is the means by which the Holy Spirit clearly reveals the most deeply hidden light of nature. This light alone opens the way to the wisdom of nature and unlocks its most complex <note>original: "abstruse"</note> mysteries."

Even the restrained imagination claimed by the author of *Remarks on Alchemy and the Alchemists* <note>Ethan Allen Hitchcock (1798–1870), a US Army officer who argued that alchemy was actually a symbolic path to spiritual salvation.</note> is likely to go astray in the labyrinth of alchemical symbolism, and some of Hitchcock’s interpretations are exceedingly forced and unnatural. He gathers citations indiscriminately from both the most spiritual <note>original: "transcendental"</note> writers and from those who, like George Starkey <note>An American colonial alchemist (1627–1665) who wrote under the pseudonym Eirenaeus Philalethes.</note>, have exhausted language in emphatic declarations that their subject and their goal are actual physical gold <note>original: "metallic gold"</note>.

*Zoroaster's Cave, or the Philosophers' Intellectual Echo to One Another from Their Caves*, is the title of a small work quoted by Hitchcock. It opens this way:

> "Dry water from the Philosophers' Clouds! Look for it and be sure to obtain it, for it is the key to inaccessible places and to those locks that would otherwise keep you out. It is of a middle nature between the fixed and the volatile <note>original: "not fixed"</note>, and partakes of a sulfurous blue color <note>original: "azurine"</note>. It is a raw, cool, feminine fire, and awaits its impregnation from a masculine solar sulfur."

Hitchcock's interpretation is this:—a pure conscience! Look for it and be sure that you have it, etc. It is of a middle nature between soul and body, and partakes of a heavenly spirit. It expects its life from God.

Needless to say, with this method, any meaning could be extracted from any allegorical writing. The

religious regeneration, we must seek the reason for existence <note>original: "raison d’être"</note> of the sublime dream of soul-chemistry <note>original: "psycho-chemistry"</note>—namely, that there is a change, a transformation <note>original: "transmutation"</note>, or a new birth possible for human beings in the flesh. This change shall clearly develop the hidden powers <note>original: "esoteric potencies"</note> of one's spiritual being, so that the flesh itself shall be purged, clarified, glorified, and clothed in the essential light of the divine spirit <note>original: "pneuma"</note>. ### THEORY AND PRACTICE OF THE GREAT WORK <note>original: "MAGNUM OPUS"</note>

only “a vapor impregnated with the metallic seed, yet undetermined, created by God Almighty, generated by the agreement and influence of the stars <note>original: "astrums"</note>, contained in the depths <note>original: "bowels"</note> of the earth, as the womb <note>original: "matrix"</note> of all created things.” 

In conformity with this, one earlier writer, Sir George Ripley, describes the stone as the potential vapor of metals. It is normally invisible, but may be made to manifest as a clear water. So also Philalethes cries in his inspired way:—“Hear me, and I shall disclose the secret, which like a rose has been guarded by thorns, so that few in past times could pull the flower. There is a substance of a metallic species, which looks so cloudy that the universe will have nothing to do with it. Its visible form is base <note>original: "vile"</note>; it defiles metallic bodies, and no one can readily imagine that the pearly drink of bright Phoebus <note>The sun; in alchemy, this often represents gold or the solar principle.</note> should spring from there. Its components are a most pure and tender mercury, and a dry, imprisoned <note>original: "incarcerate"</note> sulfur, which binds it and restrains its flow <note>original: "fluxation"</note>. . . . Know this subject, it is the sure basis of all our secrets. . . . To deal plainly, it is the child of Saturn <note>In alchemical symbolism, Saturn represents lead or the "prima materia," the raw starting material which is often described as dark and heavy.</note>, of low price and great venom. . . . It is not malleable, though metallic. Its color is black <note>original: "sable"</note> with intermixed silver <note>original: "argent"</note>, which marks the black field with veins of glittering silver.” *

The poisonous nature of the stone is much insisted on by numerous philosophers. “Its substance and its vapor are indeed a poison which the philosophers should know how to change into an antidote by preparation and direction.” †

No descriptions, supplied without limit <note>original: "*ad infinitum*"</note> by the numberless adepts who were moved by unselfish generosity to expound the secrets <note>original: "arcana"</note> of alchemy, for the spiritual, intellectual, and physical enrichment of those who deserved—

---

\* *Aphorisms of Urbigerus* <note>original: "Aphorismi Urbigerani"</note>.
† Commentary on the “Ancient War of the Knights.”

--- Page 47 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the transmutation of metals, specifically focusing on the properties of the "perfect stone" and its applications beyond simple gold-making.</meta>

->### THEORY AND PRACTICE OF THE MAGNUM OPUS <note>original: "Magnum Opus"; the "Great Work" of alchemy, the process of creating the Philosopher's Stone.</note><-

...bearing every test. The base metals used must be purified to ensure the success of the operation. The process for the production of silver is essentially similar, but the potential of the matter is not developed to such a high degree.

According to the *Commentary on the Ancient War of the Knights* <note>A significant 17th-century alchemical text often attributed to Limojon de Saint-Didier</note>, the transmutations performed by the perfect stone are so absolute that no trace remains of the original metal. It cannot, however, destroy gold, nor refine it into a more perfect metallic substance. Therefore, the stone transforms gold into a medicine a thousand times superior to any virtues which can be extracted from it in its common <note>original: "vulgar"</note> state. This medicine becomes an extremely potent agent in the refinement <note>original: "exaltation"</note> of base metals.

Among the secondary <note>original: "incidental"</note> properties of this perfect mineral agent is the conversion of flints into precious stones. However, the manufacture of gold and jewels is generally declared to be the least of the philosophical secrets. This is because the spirit which informs the mysterious *prime matter* <note>original: "prima materia"; the fundamental, formless substance from which all things were believed to originate</note> of this great and sublime work can be used and adapted in various ways to achieve absolute perfection in all the "liberal sciences." It grants the possession of the entire wisdom of nature, and of things more secret and extraordinary than the gift of prophecy—which Rhasis <note>likely Rhazes (c. 865–925), a Persian polymath and alchemist</note> and Bono <note>likely Petrus Bonus, a 14th-century Italian alchemist</note> claim is contained within the red stone.

<summary>This page explains that while the Philosopher's Stone can create gold and jewels, its highest use is as a powerful medicine and a source of universal wisdom and scientific perfection. It emphasizes that the transmutation of base metals is merely a secondary application of the "Great Work."</summary>

<keywords>Magnum Opus, Philosopher's Stone, transmutation, base metals, medicine, prima materia, Rhasis, Petrus Bonus, Ancient War of the Knights, alchemy</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Geber (Jabir ibn Hayyan), the famous Arabian alchemist, focusing on his historical reputation and the bibliography of works attributed to him.</meta>

# GEBER.

the entire circle of the physical sciences, including astronomy and medicine. In comparison, only a few fragments alone remain of all these colossal achievements. Cardan included their author among the twelve most penetrating minds of the whole world, and Boerhaave spoke of him with consideration and respect in his celebrated *Foundations of Chemistry* <note>original: "Institutiones Chemicæ"</note>. According to M. Hoefer, he deserves to be ranked first among the chemists and alchemists who flourished prior to Van Helmont.

> “He is the oracle of medieval chemists, who frequently did nothing in their writings but literally reproduce their master. Geber is to the history of chemistry what Hippocrates is to the history of medicine.”

The name of Geber has been borne or assumed by several writers subsequent to the Hermetic adept <note>A practitioner of the secret arts of alchemy and philosophy attributed to Hermes Trismegistus.</note>; in this way, the few extant facts concerning his life have been variously distorted, and books of later date and less value were falsely ascribed to him. An astronomical commentary on the *Great Treatise* <note>original: "Syntaxis Magna"; better known by its Arabic title, the Almagest.</note> of Ptolemy, in nine books, must be included in this number. It is a work of the twelfth century, as may be proved by internal evidence.

The extant works of Geber are, for the most part, in Latin, and are all open to more or less legitimate suspicion. In the library at Leyden, there are said to be several Arabic manuscripts which have never been translated, and there is one in the Imperial Library at Paris, together with a *Fragment on Spherical Triangles* <note>original: "Fragmentum de Triangulis Sphæricis"</note> which is still unprinted. The most complete edition of Geber is that of Danzig, published in 1682, and reproduced in the collection of Mangetus.* First in importance among the works of the Arabian adept must be ranked his “Sum of Perfection” — *The Four Books on the Perfection of the Mastery in its Own Nature* <note>original: "Summæ Perfectionis magisterii in suâ naturâ Libri IV."</note>. The next in value is the treatise entitled

---

* J. J. Mangetus, “*The Curious Chemical Library*” <note>original: "Bib. Chem. Curiosa"</note>, 2 volumes, folio, 1702.

--- Page 61 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of legendary alchemical figures like Morien and Kalid, then introduces the biography of the famous medieval philosopher Albertus Magnus.</meta>

<page-num>57</page-num>

Small tracts are attributed to Kalid, and also to Galip, who appears to have participated in the secret. Morien himself is cited as the author of three works, said to have been translated from the Arabic, but their authenticity is, of course, very doubtful. The first is entitled *Book on the Distinction of Mercurial Waters* <note>original: *Liber de Distinctione Mercurii Aquarum*</note>, of which a manuscript copy existed in the library of Robert Boyle <note>Robert Boyle (1627–1691) was a founding father of modern chemistry, though he also studied alchemy</note>. The second is the *Book on the Composition of Alchemy* <note>original: *Liber de Compositione Alchemiæ*</note>, printed in the first volume of the *Curious Chemical Library* <note>original: *Bibliotheca Chemica Curiosa*</note>. Finally, several editions have appeared of a treatise entitled *A Small Book on Metals, the Transmutation of Metals, and the Secret and Greatest Medicine of the Ancients* <note>original: *De Re Metallica, metallorum Transmutatione, et occulta summaque antiquorum medicina libellus*</note>. It was first printed at Paris in the year 1559.

Roger Bacon and Arnold of Villanova, who appeared one at the beginning and the other at the end of the thirteenth century, have cited Morien as an authority among the Hermetic philosophers; and Robertus Castrensis <note>also known as Robert of Chester, a 12th-century translator</note> assures us that he translated Morien’s book from the Arabic language in the year 1182.

The *Book on the Composition of Alchemy* contains a Hermetic conversation between Morien, Kalid, and Galip. It appeals to the authority of Hermes, whom it states to have been the first who discovered the <term>grand magisterium</term> <note>The "Great Work" or the secret to creating the Philosopher's Stone</note>, the secret of which he transmitted to his disciples. It declares the <term>prima materia</term> <note>The formless "first matter" that alchemists believed was the base of all substances</note> to be one, quoting the testimony of the wise king and philosopher Hercules and the adept Arsicanus, along with other false authorities. These names discredit the date of the dialogue far more than they support the alchemical theory in question.

## ALBERTUS MAGNUS.

The universal genius of Albert, joined to a commendable curiosity in so great a philosopher, says the original *Lives of Alchemical Philosophers*, did not allow him to pass by the Hermetic science without giving it due attention.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Roger Bacon’s alchemical and philosophical theories, specifically focusing on his beliefs regarding extreme longevity and his practical contributions to chemistry.</meta>

->## LIVES OF ALCHEMICAL PHILOSOPHERS<-

The healing properties speedily cured him. The experimenter examined these herbs and found them to be of a remarkably green color. On the authority of Artephius <note>A legendary alchemist to whom several influential medieval texts were attributed.</note>, he relates how a certain magician named Tantalus, who was attached to the court of the King of India, had discovered through his proficiency in astrological knowledge a method of preserving life over several centuries. He elaborates on the potency of theriac <note term="theriac">A famous ancient herbal compound used as a universal antidote and longevity tonic.</note> in the extreme prolongation of life. He also praises the flesh of winged serpents as a specific remedy against aging in mankind. Based on the health practices of Artephius, he informs us that this adept lived for over a thousand years. If Plato and Aristotle failed to prolong their own existence, it is not surprising, for they were ignorant even of the quadrature of the circle <note term="quadrature of the circle">The ancient mathematical puzzle of creating a square with the exact same area as a circle; Bacon believed it had been solved in his time.</note>, which Bacon declares to have been well known in his day, and which he considers far less significant than the grand medical doctrine of Artephius.*

The chemical investigations of the great English Franciscan friar have proved valuable to the science which he loved. He carefully studied the properties of saltpeter <note term="saltpetre">Potassium nitrate, a key ingredient in gunpowder.</note>, and if he did not discover gunpowder himself, he contributed to its perfection by teaching how to purify saltpeter by dissolving it in water and through crystallization. He also called attention to the chemical role played by the air in combustion.†

Many of Bacon’s works still remain in manuscript form, but his *The Mirror of Alchemy* <note>original: "Speculum Alchimiæ"</note> was translated into French by Girard de Tourmes and published at Lyons in small book formats in 1557. *On the Marvelous Power of Art and Nature* <note>original: "De Potestate Mirabili Artis et Naturæ"</note>, which is merely a chapter of the Letter already cited, was translated by the same person.

In another work, entitled *The Root of the World* <note>original: "Radix Mundi"</note>, the supreme secret of Hermetic philosophy is said to be hidden in the—

pleasures, and then repent of your useless attempts; mourn for the time which you have wasted in pursuing a being whom you fondly deemed perfect, but who has so dreadful a blemish! Change this useless and criminal passion into holy love, direct your affections to the Creator, not to the creature, and in the acquisition of eternal bliss, take now the same pains which you have until now vainly spent to engage me in your foolish passion!"

The sight had already melted the heart of Raymond Lully <note>Ramon Llull (c. 1232–1315) was a Majorcan philosopher, logician, and mystic often associated with alchemy in later traditions</note> and restored him to reason. After expressing to the noble-hearted lady how deeply he felt for her misfortune, he withdrew from the house, ashamed of the passion he had conceived. Reaching home, overwhelmed with confusion, he cast himself at the feet of a crucifix and vowed to consecrate himself from then on to the service of God alone. He passed a more than usually tranquil night, being filled with this zealous resolution, and it is said that a vision of Christ appeared to him, saying, "Raymond Lully, from now on, follow me!" This vision was repeated several times, and he judged it to be an indication of the Divine Will. 

Raymond was at this period about thirty years old; he held one of the most noble positions at court and might have aspired to any honor for himself or his family. He resolved, nevertheless, to renounce the world and soon arranged his affairs, dividing enough of his estate among his family to enable them to live honorably, retaining a small portion for his personal necessities, and distributing the rest among the poor. His plans in the matter were so punctually fulfilled that he was accused of plunging from one folly to another.

At this period, he is said to have made a pilgrimage to St. John <note>original: "St John in Galicia"; this most likely refers to the famous pilgrimage destination of Santiago (St. James) de Compostela</note> in Galicia and a retreat there. He returned in due course to Majorca and took the religious habit <note>i.e., joined a religious order, specifically the Third Order of St. Francis</note>, but

<meta>This page continues the biographical account of the alchemist Raymond Lully, specifically focusing on his legendary time in England and the historical inconsistencies surrounding the "Rose Nobles" story.</meta>

### RAYMOND LULLY.

His lodging in the Tower proved to be only an honorable prison. When Raymond had satisfied the desires of the King, the latter disregarded the goal which the adept <note>original: "adept"; a term for a person skilled in the secrets of alchemy</note> was so eager to see carried out. To regain his own liberty, Lully was forced to escape secretly, and he quickly departed from England.

Cremer, whose intentions were sincere, was no less grieved than Raymond at this outcome, but he was a subject to his sovereign and could only groan in silence. He declares his extreme distress in his testament, and his monastery offered up daily prayers to God for the success of Raymond's cause. The Abbot lived long after this and saw part of the reign of King Edward III. The course of operations which he proposes in his testament, with apparent sincerity, is no less veiled in mystery than those found in the most obscure authors. *

Now, in the first place, this story is not consistent with itself. If Raymond Lully were in Vienna in 1311, how did John Cremer manage to meet him in Italy at or about the same time? In the second place, the whole story concerning the manufacture of Rose Nobles <note>A famous English gold coin; legend claimed Lully created the gold for them through alchemy</note> is a series of blunders. The King who ascended the throne of England in 1307 was Edward II, but the Rose Nobles first appear in the history of numismatics <note>The study or collection of coins and currency</note> during the reign of Edward IV, in the year 1465.

"In the King's fifth year, by another indenture <term>a formal legal contract or agreement</term> with Lord Hastings, the gold coins were again altered, and it was ordered that only forty-five nobles, instead of fifty as in the last two reigns, should be made from a pound of gold. This brought back the weight of the noble to one hundred and fifty grains <note>A small unit of measurement for precious metals</note>, as it had been from 1351 to 1412, but its value was raised to 10 shillings. At the same time, new coins impressed with angels <note>The "Angel" was a gold coin introduced in 1465, depicting the archangel Michael</note> were ordered to be made, sixty—

---

\* "Lives of Alchemystical Philosophers," edited 1815.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of alchemical literature, moving from allegorical complaints about failed alchemists to the works and experiments of the 14th-century Italian monk Ferarius.</meta>

->LIVES OF ALCHEMICAL PHILOSOPHERS.<-

In "Nature's Remonstrance to the Alchemist," the alchemist is described as a foolish and deceptive *<term>souffleur</term>* <note>original: "souffleur"; literally a "bellows-blower," a derogatory term for an amateur alchemist who focused only on mechanical labor rather than philosophical understanding</note> who uses nothing but mechanical arts. The complainant—Nature herself—bitterly scolds the fanatical student who spreads the foul smells of sulfur over her beautiful domain. He tortures these materials in his furnaces in vain, for he will certainly achieve nothing by such a method. In his "Reply," the alchemist appears as a repentant man, convinced of his mistakes. He blames these errors on the crude allegories, symbolic sentences, and misleading instructions found in the writings of the master alchemists.

### THE MONK FERARIUS.

Around the beginning of the fourteenth century, this Italian artist gave the world two treatises—*On the Philosophers' Stone* <note>original: *De Lapide Philosophorum*</note> and *The Treasury of Philosophy* <note>original: *Thesaurus Philosophiæ*</note>, which are printed in *The Chemical Theater* <note>original: *Theatrum Chimicum*</note>.

The "wonderful spectacle" of the <term>palingenesis</term> <note>The supposed chemical process of resurrecting a plant or animal from its ashes.</note> of plants is described by this Jesuit. "Immediately after exposing the vial filled with the essence of the rose to the sun's rays, a perfect world of miracles is revealed within the narrow space of the vessel. The plant which lay buried in its ashes wakes up, rises, and unfolds. In the space of half an hour, the vegetable phoenix is brought back to life from its own dust. The rose emerges from its tomb and takes on a new life. It is the floral symbol of the resurrection through which mortals, lying in darkness and in the shadow of death, will pass into beautiful immortality."

The treatise on the philosophical stone very correctly observes that in alchemy, the first thing to be determined is what is truly meant by the *<term>many-named quicksilver of the wise</term>* <note>original: *myrionimous argentum vivum sapientum*</note>, a point on which the author gracefully

# NICHOLAS FLAMEL

At these words, Flamel eagerly stretched out his hands to take possession of the priceless gift, but the book and the angel disappeared in a gold-bearing <note>original: "auriferous"</note> tide of light. The scribe <note>original: "scrivener"</note> awoke to be enchanted from then on by the divine dream of <term>alchemy</term> <note>The medieval forerunner of chemistry, focused on transforming base metals into gold and finding a universal elixir.</note>. However, so much time passed without any fulfillment of the angelic promise that the passion of his imagination cooled. The great hope gradually dwindled away, and he was settling once more into the ordinary existence of a hardworking scribe when, on a certain day of election in the year 1357, an event occurred which proved the truthfulness of his visionary promise-maker and raised his ambition and aspirations to an intense heat. This event, along with the consequences it brought about, is narrated in the last testament of Nicholas Flamel, which begins in the following impressive manner, but leaves out all reference to the legendary vision:

> "The Lord God of my life, who raises the humble in spirit out of the lowest dust and makes the hearts of those who hope in Him rejoice, be eternally praised.
>

> 
> "In Him let us always put our confidence, in His fear let us place our happiness, and in His mercy the hope and glory of restoration from our fallen state.
> 
> "And in our prayers <note>original: "supplications"</note> to Him, let us show a faith that is sincere <note>original: "unfeigned"</note> and firm, an assurance that shall not be shaken forever.
> 
> "And You, O Lord God Almighty, as You, out of Your infinite and most desirable goodness, have condescended to open the earth and unlock Your treasures to me, Your poor and unworthy servant, and have given into my possession the sources and springs of all the treasures and riches of this world."

<meta>This page continues Nicholas Flamel's autobiographical account, describing how he earned his living as a scribe in Paris and his discovery of the mysterious alchemical book that would define his life's work.</meta>

# NICHOLAS FLAMEL

I earned my living by the art of writing, drafting legal documents <note>original: "engrossing inventories"</note>, making up accounts, keeping books, and similar work.

“During this time, a gilded book, very old and large, fell into my hands by chance. It cost me only two *florins* <note>A gold coin used in various European countries; two florins was a modest but significant sum for a scribe at the time.</note>.

“It was not made of paper or parchment like other books, but of remarkable bark <note>original: "admirable rinds"</note> (as it seemed to me) from young trees. Its cover was made of *brass*; it was well bound and engraved all over with strange letters, which I took to be Greek characters or something similar.

“I only know that I could not read them; they were neither Latin nor French letters, both of which I understand somewhat.

“Regarding the content written inside, it was engraved (as I suppose) with an iron stylus <note>original: "pencil or graver"</note> upon the bark leaves. It was done remarkably well, in beautiful and neat Latin letters, and elaborately colored.

“It contained twenty-one leaves <note>original: "thrice seven leaves"</note>, for they were numbered at the top of each page <note>original: "folio"</note>. Every seventh leaf had no writing on it; instead, several images or figures were painted there.

“On the first seventh leaf was depicted: 1. A Virgin. 2. Serpents swallowing her up. On the second seventh leaf, a serpent crucified; and on the last seventh leaf, a desert or wilderness, in the middle of which were many beautiful fountains, from which a number of serpents emerged here and there.

“On the first leaf was written in gold capital letters: 'Abraham the Jew, Priest, Prince, Levite, Astrologer, and Philosopher, to the nation of the Jews dispersed throughout France by the wrath of God, wishes health.'

“After these words, the page was filled with many curses and threats, including the word <term>MARANATHA</term> <note>original: "Maranatha"—an Aramaic phrase meaning "Our Lord has come" or "O Lord, come!"; in this context, it is used as a solemn warning or curse against those who would profane a sacred text.</note>, which was frequently repeated against anyone who might look inside to reveal its secrets, unless he was either a Priest or a Scribe.”

<summary>Nicholas Flamel describes his professional life as a scribe and his chance purchase of a mysterious, ancient brass-bound book written by "Abraham the Jew." The book's unusual construction and symbolic illustrations of serpents and a crucified snake suggest it contains deep alchemical secrets.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Nicholas Flamel's account of discovering a mysterious alchemical book. Having previously described the book's physical appearance—made of bark and bound in brass—he now details its contents and the spiritual and practical instructions it contained for the Jewish people.</meta>

->LIVES OF ALCHEMISTICAL PHILOSOPHERS.<-

"The person who sold me this book was as ignorant of its worth as I was when I bought it. I judge it might have been stolen from some of the Jewish nation, or else found in some place where they used to live in ancient times.

"On the second page <note>original: "leaf"</note> of the book, the author consoled his nation and gave them godly advice to turn from their wickedness and evil ways. Above all, he urged them to flee from idolatry and to wait in patience for the coming of the Messiah, who, by conquering all the kings and powerful rulers <note>original: "potentates"</note> of the earth, would reign in glory with his people for eternity. Without doubt, this was a very godly, wise, and understanding man.

"On the third page, and in all the writings that followed, he taught them, in plain words, the <term>transmutation of metals</term> <note>The alchemical process of changing "base" metals like lead into "noble" metals like gold.</note>. He did this so that he might help and assist his scattered people to pay their taxes <note>original: "tribute"</note> to the Roman Emperors, along with some other things not necessary to repeat here.

"He painted the vessels <note>The glass containers, like flasks or retorts, used in alchemical experiments.</note> along the side or margin of the pages and revealed all the colors as they should arise or appear, along with all the rest of the work.

"But regarding the <term>first matter</term> <note>original: "*prima materia*"; the base substance required to begin the alchemical process.</note>, or the primary agent, he did not say so much as a single word. He only told them that on the fourth and fifth pages he had entirely painted or illustrated it, depicting it with great skill and craftsmanship.

"Now, although it was exceptionally well and clearly <note>original: "materially or intelligibly"</note> illustrated and painted, no man could have ever been able to understand it without being well-skilled in their <term>Kabbalah</term> <note>original: "Cabala"; a system of Jewish mystical and esoteric interpretation.</note>, which is a series of ancient traditions, and also without having studied their books extensively.

"The fourth and fifth pages were without any writing, but were full of beautiful figures, bright and shining, or, as it were, illuminated, and very exquisitely depicted."

> “These I showed to the greatest scholars and most learned men in **Paris**, who understood no more of them than I did. I told them the figures were found in a book which taught the secret of the <term>philosophers’ stone</term> <note>A legendary alchemical substance capable of turning base metals into gold and sometimes said to be an elixir of life.</note>.”

<meta>This page continues Nicholas Flamel's narrative of his struggle to understand the "Book of Abraham the Jew." Having shared the book's contents with his wife, Perrenelle, Flamel now describes his attempts to seek outside help and the subsequent years he spent following a false lead.</meta>

<page-num>103</page-num>

# NICHOLAS FLAMEL.

“However, most of them mocked both me and that most excellent secret, except for one man named Anselm, a practitioner of medicine <note>original: "physic"</note> and a serious student of this art.

“He greatly desired to see my book, which he valued more than anything else in the world, but I always refused him, only giving him a lengthy explanation of the method.

“He told me that the first figure represented Time, which devours all things, and that, according to the number of the six written leaves, a period of six years was required to perfect the stone; and then, he said, we must turn the hourglass and look at it no more.

“I told him this was not painted merely for decoration, but to show the teacher the **primary matter** <term>prima materia</term> <note>The foundational, formless substance that alchemists believed was necessary to begin the creation of the Philosophers' Stone.</note>, or 'first agent,' as was written in the book. He answered me that this 'digestion' <note>The alchemical process of heating a substance slowly over a long period.</note> for six years was, as it were, a second agent, and that the first agent was certainly painted there, which was a white and heavy water.

“This, he claimed, was without doubt **quicksilver** <term>argent vive</term> <note>original: "argent vive" — literally "living silver"; mercury.</note>, which they could not 'fix'—that is, stop its movement or take away its fluidity <note>original: "volubility"</note>—except by that long digestion in the pure blood of young infants.

“For once this **quicksilver** <note>original: "argent vive"</note> was joined with **Gold** <term>Sol</term> <note>The alchemical symbol and name for gold, associated with the Sun.</note> and **Silver** <term>Luna</term> <note>The alchemical symbol and name for silver, associated with the Moon.</note>, it was first turned with them into a plant, like the one painted there, and afterwards through decomposition <note>original: "corruption"</note> into serpents. These serpents, once perfectly dried and digested, were made into a fine powder of gold, which is the stone.

“This discourse, which was irrelevant <note>original: "strange or foreign"</note> to the true matter, was the cause of my error. It made me wander for twenty-one years in a complete maze, far from the truth. During that time, I went through a thousand different processes <note>original: "labyrinths or processes"</note>, but all in vain. However, I never worked with the blood of infants, for I considered that to be wicked and villainous.”

“I found in my book that the philosophers called the 'mineral spirit'—which is found in metals, primarily in **Gold** <term>Sol</term>, **Silver** <term>Luna</term>, and **Mercury** <note>original: 'Sol, Luna, and Mercury'</note>—'blood.' In my own judgment, I always agreed with this interpretation. However, these explanations were, for the most part, more clever than they were accurate <note>original: 'not more subtle than true'</note>.

“Because I did not find the signs described in my book appearing at the expected times during my operations or process, I was constantly forced to start over.

“Eventually, having lost all hope of ever understanding those symbols or illustrations, I made a vow to God to seek their interpretation from some Jewish priest belonging to a synagogue in Spain.

“With the consent of my wife, Perrenelle, I took a copy of the illustrations and set off. I dressed in a pilgrim’s robe and carried a staff, just as you see me depicted outside the arch in the churchyard where I placed these Hieroglyphic Figures <note>Flamel refers to the Cemetery of the Innocents in Paris, which he famously decorated with alchemical allegories.</note>.

“On that same wall, on both sides, I have displayed the process, showing in order all the colors of the [Philosophers'] stone as they appear and then disappear.

“This is, in a sense, the very beginning of the book by Hercules titled *Iris, or the Rainbow*, which discusses the stone in these words: *The process of the work is very pleasing to nature.*

“I included these words there specifically for the benefit of great scholars and learned men, who may understand what they allude to.

“In this way, I began my journey to Spain. I traveled so efficiently that I soon arrived at Mountjoy <note>Mons Jovis, a common stop for pilgrims</note> and, shortly after, at St. James <note>Santiago de Compostela, the famous pilgrimage site</note>, where I devoutly fulfilled my vow.

“Having done this, I was in León on my return journey when I met a merchant from Boulogne. He introduced me to a physician named Master Canches <note>original: 'M. Canches'</note>, a man of Jewish heritage who had converted to Christianity and was living in León.

Despite this, he was in constant fear that I should leave or forsake him, which was a great trouble to him. And although I was continually by his side, he would almost always be calling for me. At the end of the seventh day of his sickness he died, which was a great grief to me. I buried him, as well as my financial condition would permit, in a church at Orleans.

"Well, I now possessed the *prima materia* <note>original: "first matter"—the formless, original substance required to begin the alchemical process</note>, the first principles; yet I did not yet have their initial preparation, which is a task more difficult than anything else in the world. During that time, I did nothing but study, search, and labor, just as you see me depicted outside this arch where I have placed my process. I also prayed continually to God and read my book attentively, pondering the words of the philosophers and then testing the various operations I thought they might have meant. Once I had this, I easily accomplished the <term>magistery</term> <note>The "magistery" refers to the successful completion of the "Great Work," or the creation of the Philosopher's Stone.</note>. For by knowing the preparations of the primary agents and then following the directions in my book literally, I could not have failed the work even if I had wanted to.

"Having achieved this, I come now to the stage of projection <note>The final alchemical process of casting the Philosopher's Stone upon a base metal to transmute it into silver or gold</note>. The first time I performed a projection was upon mercury; I transformed about a pound and a half of it into pure silver, which was better than silver from a mine. I proved this by testing it myself and having others assay <term>test for purity</term> it for me several times. This was done in the year of our Lord 1382, on January 17, at about noon on a Monday, in my own house, with only Perrenelle <note>Flamel's wife and collaborator</note> present.

> “I speak this in all truth: I have performed this three times with the help of Perrenelle, who understood the process as well as I did because she assisted me in my operations. For an extremity of joy takes away one's reason just as much as an extremity of grief and sorrow. But the goodness of the most great God had not only blessed me by giving me a chaste and sober wife, but she was also a wise and prudent woman. She was not only capable of reason but also of acting reasonably, and she was more discreet and secretive than women usually are. Above all, she was exceedingly religious and devout. Therefore, seeing that she had no hope of having children and was now <term>well stricken in years</term> <note>An archaic phrase meaning "advanced in age."</note>, she made it her business, as I did, to think of God and to devote ourselves to works of charity and mercy.
