It was at the end of the year of our Lord 1413. After the death of my faithful companion <note>Perrenelle, Flamel's wife and alchemical partner</note>, whose loss I cannot help but mourn every day of my life, she and I had already founded and endowed with income fourteen hospitals, three chapels, and seven churches in the city of Paris. We had rebuilt all of these from the ground up and enriched them with great gifts and funding, along with many repairs to their cemeteries. We also did about as much in Boulogne as we did in Paris, not to mention the charitable deeds we both performed for specific poor people, especially widows and orphans. If I were to disclose their names, the scale of the charity, and our methods of doing it, my reward would only be in this world; furthermore, it might not be pleasing to the people we helped.

> “Therefore, while building these hospitals, chapels, churches, and cemeteries in the city, I had the most true and essential marks or signs of this art <term>the art: alchemy</term> depicted under the fourth arch. These were presented under veils, types, and hieroglyphic symbols, in imitation of the things contained in the gilded book of Abraham the Jew. This demonstrates to wise men of understanding the direct and perfect way to operate, and the step-by-step process <note>original: "lineary work"</note> of the philosopher's stone. Once someone has perfected this stone, it removes from them the root of all sin and evil—which is greed <note>original: "covetousness"</note>—changing their evil into good and making them generous, polite, religious, devout, and God-fearing, no matter how wicked they were before. For from then on, they are continually overwhelmed by the goodness of God and by the grace and mercy they have obtained from the fountain of Eternal Goodness, the depth of His divine and adorable power, and the consideration of His wonderful works.”

<meta>This page continues the biography of the French alchemist Nicholas Flamel, following the death of his wife Perrenelle, and details his final years, his literary legacy, and his last will and testament.</meta>

<page-num>110</page-num>

-># LIVES OF ALCHEMICAL PHILOSOPHERS.<-

After the death of Perrenelle, the bereaved adept is supposed to have prepared for posterity several works on the supreme science which had enriched him: *The Book of Hieroglyphic Figures* <note>original: *Le Livre des Figures Hieroglyphiques*</note>; *The Philosophical Summary* <note>original: *Le Sommaire Philosophique*</note>, written in verse after the style of the *Romance of the Rose*; *Three Treatises on Metallic Transformation* <note>original: *Trois Traités de la Transformation Metallique*</note>, also in rhymed verse; *The Desired Desire, or Treasure of Philosophy* <note>original: *Le Desir Désiré, ou Trésor de Philosophie*</note>; *The Great Clarification of the Philosopher’s Stone for the Transmutation of all Metals* <note>original: *Le Grand Eclaircissement de la Pierre Philosophale pour la Transmutation de tous Métaux*</note>; *Chemical Music* <note>original: *La Musique Chimique*</note>; and *Annotations on D. Zacharius, etc.* <note>original: *Annotationes in D. Zacharmin, &c.*</note>

Approaching the end of his life, and having no children, he chose his burial place in the parish church of St. Jacques-la-Boucherie, in front of the crucifix. To this end, he made a contract with the wardens of the church, which is mentioned in his testament. He then disposed of his property and goods to the church and to the poor, as may be seen in his will, which is kept in the archives of St. Jacques. It is dated November 22, 1416, and begins as follows:

> “To all those to whom these present letters shall come, I, Annegny du Castel, knight <note>original: *chevalier*</note>, counselor and chamberlain <note>original: *chambellan*</note> of the King, our Master, Keeper of the Provost <note>original: *Prevot*</note> of Paris, greetings: Know ye, that before Hugues de la Barre and Jean de la Noe, notary clerks of the King at the Châtelet, there appeared personally Nicholas Flamel, <term>scrivener</term> <note>a professional scribe, notary, or clerk</note>, sound in body and mind, speaking clearly, with good and true understanding,” etc.

The document fills four sheets of parchment, which are sewn together end-to-end like ancient scrolls. It contains thirty-four articles; in the twentieth, he bequeaths to his relatives the sum of forty livres <note>a French unit of currency</note>. He lived three years after making this will, dying about 1419.

"It teaches the manner of accomplishing the *Great Work* <note>original: "Magnum Opus"</note> by the aid of successive operations, which are termed *washings* <note>original: "Lavures"</note> in this treatise. On the last leaf of the manuscript is the following inscription written by the same hand as the rest of the text:—' This book belongs to Nicolas Flamel, of the Parish of Saint-Jacques-de-la-Boucherie, who has written and illuminated <note>decorated with gold, silver, or brilliant colors</note> it with his own hand.' With regard to the extent of the scribe’s resources, the genuine testament of Pernelle, dated 1399, and the endowments of hospitals and churches which undoubtedly took place on a scale of great generosity, are sufficient evidence that he was an exceedingly wealthy man.

Other critics, including Louis Figuier, admit the fact of his riches but expand upon the profitable nature of a scribe's occupation before the invention of printing, and upon the careful frugality of the supposed <term>alchemist</term> <note>a practitioner of the medieval chemical science aiming to achieve the transmutation of base metals into gold</note>. However, despite their own theory, they are obliged to admit that Flamel did become a student of alchemy; that the hieroglyphics, figures, and emblems in the Cemetery of the Holy Innocents are evidence of this fact; that, unlike most followers of Hermes, he was not impoverished by his experiments; and that he encouraged the report that his wealth was mainly a result of his possession of the mysterious book of Abraham, by which he had been able to create the <term>philosophical stone</term> <note>a legendary substance capable of turning base metals into gold and providing the elixir of life</note>.

Gabriel Naudé, who detested magic and seems to have despised alchemy—insulting the practitioners of both these sciences alike—accounts for Flamel's riches by asserting that he managed business affairs for the Jews. Upon their banishment from the kingdom of France and the confiscation of their property for the king, "he, knowing...

--- Page 117 ---
<meta>This page continues the investigation into the origins of Nicholas Flamel's wealth, specifically addressing the theory that he profited from the persecution of the Jewish community in Paris.</meta>

### NICHOLAS FLAMEL

"...the sums owed by several individuals, he settled <note>original: "compromised"</note> by receiving a portion, which they paid him to prevent him from providing information that would force them to surrender the money entirely."

This explanation for the source of Flamel’s riches is a completely unfounded assertion. If we carefully examine history, there were three expulsions of the Jewish people from France between 1300 and 1420. They were banished in 1308, were allowed to return soon after, and were banished again in 1320. These persecutions occurred before Flamel was born. The Jewish community was re-established by Charles V in 1364, and they remained in peace until the riots that occurred in Paris in 1380, at the beginning of the reign of Charles VI. At that time, the public rose up against the Jews, committing great atrocities and demanding their expulsion. The uprising <note>original: "sedition"</note>, however, was suppressed, and the Jewish people were protected until 1393. In that year, following several charges brought against them, they were ordered to leave France or convert to Christianity. 

The historian Mezeray <note>François Eudes de Mézeray (1610–1683), a prominent French historian</note> says that some of them chose to abandon their religion rather than the kingdom, but others sold their property and departed <note>original: "retired"</note>. Thus, it appears that the only expulsion of the Jews that could align with Naudé’s <note>Gabriel Naudé (1600–1653), a scholar and librarian who often sought to debunk supernatural claims</note> theory <note>original: "surmise"</note> occurred without the seizure <note>original: "confiscation"</note> of their property. Therefore, it could not have given Flamel the opportunity alleged—if, indeed, it were even reasonable to suppose that all the Parisian Jews <note>original: "Israelites"</note> entrusted their affairs to a single person when there is no evidence that such an agent was necessary. There is, therefore, no reason to suppose that Flamel was enriched by the property of the Jews, or that those who owed them money settled their debts <note>original: "compounded"</note> with Flamel to prevent him from denouncing them to the king.*

---

* According to Louis Figuier <note>a 19th-century French writer who popularized science and history</note>, there were two minor persecutions of the Jews: one in 1346, when Flamel was merely a boy, and the other in 1354, when he had barely established his business.

He spoke to me in Italian, but he soon discovered by my accent that it was not my native language, and asked me frankly what country I came from. As soon as he knew that I was a native of France, he spoke to me in as good French as if he had been raised in Paris. "How long, sir," I asked, "did you stay in France?" He replied he had never been there, but that he had a great desire to undertake the journey.

> "I did all in my power to encourage that resolution and to convince him that France was the nursery <note>original: "nursery of the learned"; meaning a place where scholarship is nurtured and fostered.</note> of the learned, and its king a patron of the sciences, who paid the expenses of my travels for collecting records of antiquities, drawings of monuments, correcting maps, and making a collection of ancient coins, manuscripts, etc., all of which he seemed to politely approve. Our conversation being ended, the dervishes <note>Members of a Muslim religious order known for their vows of poverty and austerity; some were believed to possess secret wisdom.</note> brought us to their house at the foot of the mountain, where, having drunk coffee, I took my leave, but with a promise, however, that I would shortly come and see them again.

> "On the 10th, the dervish whom I took for an Uzbek <note>original: "Usbec"</note> came to pay me a visit. I showed him all the manuscripts I had bought, and he assured me they were very valuable and written by great authors. He was a man every way extraordinary in learning; and in external appearance, he seemed to be about thirty years old, but from his conversation, I was persuaded he had lived a century.

> "He told me he was one of seven friends who traveled to perfect their studies and, every twenty years, met in a place previously appointed. I perceived that Bursa <note>original: "Bronosa"; referring to the city in modern-day Turkey.</note> was the place of their present meeting, and that four of them had arrived. Religion and natural philosophy <note>The study of nature and the physical universe, the precursor to modern science.</note> occupied our thoughts by turns; and at last, we fell upon chemistry, alchemy, and the Kabbalah <note>original: "Cabala"; an ancient tradition of mystical interpretation of the Bible.</note>. I told him all these, and especially the philosophers’ stone <note>A legendary alchemical substance capable of turning base metals into gold and providing the "elixir of life" for immortality.</note>, were regarded by most men of sense as mere fictions."

### BASIL VALENTINE.

One of the most illustrious of the adept philosophers is undoubtedly Basil Valentine (Basilius Valentinus), who was born at Mainz <note>original: "Mayence"</note> and became the prior of St. Peter's at Erfurt in 1414. His name was suspected to be a pseudonym used to conceal the identity of some skilled practitioner <note>original: "accomplished artist"; in alchemy, "artist" refers to one who practices the "Great Work"</note>, but the history of the city of Erfurt, published by J. M. Gudemus, assures us of the philosopher's existence and name based on public records. Gudemus shows us that in 1413 he was a resident of the monastic house already mentioned and that he distinguished himself through a profound knowledge of nature. <note>original Latin footnote: *Eadem ætate (scilicet anno 1413) Basilius Valentinus in divi Patri monasteris vixit arte medica et naturale indagatione admirabilis.* Translation: "At that same time (namely in the year 1413), Basil Valentine lived in the monastery of Saint Peter, remarkable for his medical skill and his investigation of nature."</note> Since the work of Gudemus was printed in 1675, the accuracy <note>original: "veracity"</note> of the *Dictionary of the Occult Sciences* (*Dictionnaire des Sciences Occultes*)—a work written in the interests of religion and for the purpose of discrediting <note>original: "blackening"</note> the secret sciences—may be judged by the following passage: “His life is so mixed with fables that some have disbelieved in his existence. He is portrayed as flourishing in the twelfth, thirteenth, fourteenth, and fifteenth centuries; it is even added, *without the smallest proof*, that he was a Benedictine at Erfurt.”

According to Olaus Borrichius, Valentine hid his writings inside one of the pillars of the abbey church. They remained in this hiding place for many years, but were at length

...discovered by the lucky strike of a lightning bolt. He was the first person to introduce antimony into medicine, and it is said that he originally tested the effects of antimonial medicines upon the monks of his convent. These medicines acted upon them with such excessive violence "that he was led to name the mineral from which these medicines had been extracted *antimoine* — meaning 'hostile to monks' <note>original French: "antimoine"</note>." However, Thomson, who tells this story in his *History of Chemistry*, shows how unlikely it is; the works of Basil Valentine, and specifically his *The Triumphant Chariot of Antimony* <note>original Latin: "Currus Triumphalis Antimonii"</note>, were written in German <note>original: "High Dutch"</note>. In his time, the German name for antimony was *speissglas* <note>original German: "speissglas"</note> and not *antimoine*, which is French.

Basil Valentine attacks the physicians of his time with the same intensity as Paracelsus <note>Paracelsus (1493–1541) was a famous physician and alchemist known for his fiery personality and rejection of traditional medicine</note>. The most ancient systems of chemical philosophy are preserved in his experiments. He praises antimony as an excellent medicine for those who are familiar with alchemical secrets. To others, it is a poison of the most powerful kind.

No further details of the life of Basil Valentine have been passed down to future generations. Numerous works have been printed in his name, though the authenticity of several is doubtful. He wrote in German, and relatively few of his treatises have been translated into other languages. The best are as follows:

1. *On the Microcosm, the Great Mystery of the World, and the Medicine of Man* <note>original Latin: "De Microcosmo deque Magno Mundi Mysterio et Medecina Hominis"</note>, Marpurg, 1609, octavo;

7. *A Chemical-Philosophical Treatise on Natural and Supernatural Things, Metals, and Minerals* <note>original Latin: "Tractatus Chimico-Philosophicus de Rebus Naturalibus et Præternaturalibus metallorum et mineralium"</note>, Frankfurt, 1676, octavo; 
8. *Haliographia, on the preparation—* <note>original Latin: "Haliographia, de præpara-"; Haliographia refers to the description of salts</note>

## BERNARD TRÉVISAN.

Bernard, Count of the March of Trévisan <note>original: "Bernard Compte de la Marche Trévisane"</note>, is credited by the popular legends of France with the powers of a sorcerer in possession of a "devil's bird" or familiar spirit; nevertheless, he is called “the good” and enjoyed a particular reputation for benevolence.

A descendant of a distinguished family from Padua, Bernard Trévisan began to study the time-honored science of alchemy around the same time that Basil <note>likely referring to Basil Valentine</note> in Germany and the two Isaacs <note>Isaac Hollandus and his son</note> in Holland were pursuing their labors with supposed success. His father was a physician in Padua, where Bernard himself was born in the year 1406. The account of his alchemical errors must rank among the most curious anecdotes in the annals of occult chemistry.

At the age of fourteen, under the sponsorship of his grandfather and with the full consent of his family, he devoted his attention to alchemy, which from then on became the absorbing occupation of his life. Seeking initiation into the first principles of the art, he began by studying Geber and Rhasis <note>referring to Jabir ibn Hayyan and Al-Razi, influential early Islamic alchemists</note>, believing they would provide him with a method of multiplying his inheritance <note>original: "patrimony"</note> a hundredfold. The experiments which he undertook during his costly instruction by these authoritative <note>original: "oracular"</note> masters resulted in the useless waste <note>original: "futile dissipation"</note> of eight hundred—or, according to another account, three thousand—crowns. He was surrounded by pretended philosophers who, finding him wealthy and eager to penetrate these tantalizing mysteries, offered...

At the end of this period, the seeker was rewarded only by a <term>quartan fever</term> <note>A form of malaria where the fever returns every four days.</note> which consumed him for fourteen months and almost cost him his life.

He had hardly recovered his health when he heard from a clerk that Maître Henry, the confessor of the German Emperor Frederick III, was in possession of the philosopher's stone. He immediately set out for Germany, accompanied by some frustrated <term>sons of Hermes</term> <note>A traditional name for alchemists, who looked to the legendary Hermes Trismegistus as their founding figure.</note> like himself. They managed, **through great means and great friends** <note>original: "par grands moyens et grands amis"</note>, to be introduced to the confessor and began to work alongside him. Bernard contributed ten marks of silver, and the others contributed thirty-two, to cover the essential expenses of the process. This process consisted of combining mercury, silver, olive oil, and sulfur. The entire mixture was dissolved over a moderate fire and stirred continually. After two months, it was placed in a glass vial, which they covered with clay and then with hot ashes. Lead, dissolved in a crucible, was added after three weeks, and the product of this melting was subjected to refinement. At the end of these operations, the imperial confessor expected that the silver used in the mixture would have increased by at least a third; on the contrary, however, it was reduced to a fourth of its original amount.

Bernard Trévisan, in utter despair, determined to abandon all further experiments. His family applauded this resolution, but within two months, the <term>Circean</term> <note>Refers to the enchantress Circe from Greek mythology; here meaning an irresistible or intoxicating power.</note> power of secret chemistry had re-established its former command over the whole being of its martyr. In a fever of eagerness, he restarted his travels and visited Spain, Italy, England, Scotland, Holland, Germany, and France. Then, anxious to drink at the Eastern fountains of alchemy, he spent several years in Egypt, Persia, and

<meta>This page continues the biographical account of the English alchemist Thomas Norton, author of the "Ordinal of Alchemy," focusing on his reputation, family, and the nature of his alchemical teachings.</meta>

# LIVES OF ALCHEMICAL PHILOSOPHERS

It is doubtful whether Thomas Norton ever enjoyed the fruits of his supposed knowledge. He does not speak of his own transmutations <note>The alchemical process of changing base metals into gold or silver.</note>, and if he is called by one of his contemporaries **the most skilled alchemist of his time** <note>original: *alchemista suo tempore peritissimus*</note>, by others he is termed a **maker of trifles in a frivolous science** <note>original: *Nugarum opifex in frivola scientia*</note>. The latter critics declare that he ruined himself by his labors, and that all his friends who trusted him with their money were as much ruined as he was. According to Fuller <note>Thomas Fuller (1608–1661), a historian famous for his biographical dictionary, "Worthies of England."</note>, he lived and died very poor; nevertheless, his family appears to have been held in high repute under King Henry VIII. There were nine brothers with the name of Norton. One anonymous writer asserts that they were all knights. The tomb of Sampson Norton, master of the king’s ordnance <note>The official in charge of the royal artillery and weaponry.</note>, who was buried in Fulham Church, was adorned with Hermetic <note>Related to Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary founder of alchemy; often refers to symbolic or alchemical art.</note> paintings according to one account. However, Faulkner, in his historical account of Fulham, describes it as a rich Gothic monument, ornamented with foliage and oak leaves, and bearing an obliterated inscription.

Thomas Norton died in 1477. His grandson Samuel followed in his steps as an alchemist and was the author of several Hermetic treatises, which are not very highly esteemed.

> *The Ordinal of Alchemy* testifies that the stone <note>The "Philosopher's Stone," the legendary substance capable of transmutation.</note> is one. In appearance, it is a subtle earth, brown and opaque; it withstands the fire and is considered to be of no value. There is also another and glorious stone, which is termed the **philosophical magnesia** <note>In alchemy, "magnesia" did not refer to the chemical element magnesium, but rather a symbolic "mother" substance or a specific stage of the prime matter.</note>. Alchemy is a wonderful science, a secret philosophy, a unique grace and free gift of the Almighty, which was never discovered by independent human labor, but only by revelation or the instruction of one of the adepts <note>An "adept" is someone who has mastered the secrets of alchemy.</note>.

# PARACELSUS.

Philippus Aureolus Theophrastus Bombast von Hohenheim was born in the year 1493, at Maria Einsiedeln, in the canton of Zurich, Switzerland. He was descended from the ancient and honorable family of Bombast, which had lived for many generations at the Castle of Hohenheim near Stuttgart, Würtemberg. His father was a physician of high reputation and possessed a large collection of rare books. His mother had been the matron of a hospital, and Theophrastus, their only child, was born one year after their marriage. It is said that he was castrated in his infancy, a tradition which may have been invented to account for his beardless and feminine appearance, and for his reported hatred of women.

Paracelsus received the first basics of his education from his father. As he advanced in his studies and abilities, he was instructed in alchemy, surgery, and medicine. One of the works of Isaac Holland <note>Isaac Holland (and his son John Isaac) were influential 15th-century Dutch alchemists whose writings focused on the philosopher's stone</note> fell into his hands, and from that moment he was driven by the ambition of curing diseases with medicines superior to the medical materials <note>original: "materia" (short for *materia medica*, the standard pharmaceutical substances of the time)</note> then in use. <insert>[</insert> He performed several chemical operations according to the books of the celebrated Hollander and adopted from his writings the ancient principles that salt, mercury, and sulfur form a "trinity" in every substance. <insert>]</insert> This system he expanded and explained through his own intellectual insights. He absorbed much of his father's extensive learning and then continued his studies under the guidance of monks in the Convent of Saint Andrew of

<summary>This page introduces the early life and education of Paracelsus, born in 1493, detailing his family background and his early fascination with the alchemical theories of Isaac Holland.</summary>
<keywords>Paracelsus, alchemy, medicine, Isaac Holland, Maria Einsiedeln, transmutation, salt-mercury-sulfur trinity, history of science</keywords>

<meta>This page concludes the biographical sketch of Paracelsus and begins the account of Denis Zachaire.</meta>

->LIVES OF ALCHEMICAL PHILOSOPHERS<-

at the age of forty-eight years and three days, in a small room of the ‘White Horse’ Inn, near the quay, and his body was buried in the graveyard of St. Sebastian.” His death is supposed to have been hastened by a scuffle with assassins in the pay of the traditional medical establishment <note>original: "orthodox medical faculty"</note>.

The most recent commentator on Paracelsus, Dr. Franz Hartmann, has devoted a chapter to the alchemical and astrological teachings of the visionary <note>original: "seer"</note> of Hohenheim. The first art, according to Paracelsus, separates the pure from the impure and develops species out of primordial matter <term>primordial matter: the fundamental, formless substance from which all things were thought to be created</term>. It perfects what Nature has left imperfect; therefore, its principles are of universal application and are not restricted to the metallic and mineral kingdoms. Gold can be made by physical chemistry, but the process is poor and unproductive in comparison with the gold which can be produced by an exercise of the occult powers which exist in the soul of man. Actual and material gold can be manufactured "psycho-chemically." By this amazing theory, Paracelsus created a new school of alchemy which abandoned experimental research and sought within themselves the secret, subject, and end of alchemical philosophy.

### DENIS ZACHAIRE.

It appears that the true name of this persevering and indefatigable seeker after the goal and truth of alchemy has not in reality come down to us; the name placed at the head of his *Little Work on Chemistry* <note>original: *Opusculum Chimicum*</note> is simply a pseudonym. It is to this little work that we are indebted for one of the most singular histories in the annals of the Hermetic art <term>Hermetic art: the practice of alchemy, named after the legendary founder Hermes Trismegistus</term>.

I remained at Toulouse all winter waiting for him, and I might have been waiting there still, as I have never heard from him since.

In the following summer, the city being visited by the plague, I went to Cahors and stayed there for six months. I did not lose sight of my work and became acquainted with an old man who was called "the philosopher"—a name given in that region to anyone of superior information. I shared my methods with him and asked for his advice. He mentioned ten or twelve processes which he thought were better than others. I returned to Toulouse when the plague ended and renewed my labors accordingly. The only consequence was that my money was all spent, except for one hundred and seventy crowns. 

To continue my operations with more certainty, I made the acquaintance of an <term>abbé</term> <note>original: abbé; a title for a French Catholic clergyman</note> who lived in the neighborhood of this city. He was seized with a passion for the same pursuit as myself, and he informed me that one of his friends, who lived with Cardinal Armanac, had sent him a process from Rome which he believed was genuine, but it would cost two hundred crowns. I agreed to provide half of this sum, and he gave the rest, so we began to work together. Our process required a large supply of spirits of wine <note>original: spirits of wine; high-proof alcohol used as a solvent</note>. I purchased a cask of excellent wine, from which I distilled the spirit and purified it many times. We took two pounds in weight of it and half a pound of gold, which we had calcined <note>original: calcined; heated until reduced to a powder</note> for a month. These were placed in a <term>pelican</term> <note>original: pelican; an alchemical flask with two curved necks that allow distilled vapors to cycle back into the main body</note> and placed in a furnace. This work lasted a year, but, so as not to remain idle, we performed some other...

"The year 1537 passed without any change appearing in the results of our labors. We might have continued this way for our entire lives, for we should have known that the "perfect metals" <note>original: perfect metals; gold and silver, which alchemists believed were resistant to change by lesser substances</note> are not altered by vegetable or animal substances. We took out our powder and performed a <term>projection</term> <note>original: projection; the final stage of the alchemical process where the "stone" or powder is cast upon molten base metal to transform it into gold</note> onto hot quicksilver, but it was in vain! Imagine then our grief, especially since the <term>abbé</term> <note>original: abbé; the head of a monastery or a title for a clergyman</note> had notified all his monks that they would have to melt down the lead cistern of their house so that he could convert it into gold as soon as our operations were finished.

"My failure could not make me give up. I again raised four hundred crowns against my property; the <term>abbé</term> did the same, and I set out for Paris, a city containing more <term>alchemists</term> than any other in the world. I resolved to stay there as long as the eight hundred crowns lasted, or until I succeeded in my object. This journey brought me the displeasure of my relatives and the criticism of my friends, who imagined I was a dedicated law student. However, I made them believe that the purpose of my stay in Paris was the purchase of a position in the law courts.

"After traveling for fifteen days, I arrived in Paris in January 1539. I remained almost unknown for a month, but as soon as I visited the furnace makers and spoke with some amateurs, I became acquainted with more than a hundred 'artists' <note>original: artists; here referring to practitioners of the "Hermetic Art" or alchemy</note> who were all at work in different ways. Some labored to extract the <term>mercury of metals</term> <note>original: mercury of metals; a theoretical "first matter" or essential spirit believed to be hidden within physical metals</note> and afterwards to 'fix' it. Others held a variety of different systems, and scarcely a day passed in which some of them did not visit me—even on Sundays and the most sacred festivals of the Church—to hear what I had done."

## BERIGARD OF PISA.

The following account of a transmutation performed by himself is recorded by the celebrated Italian philosopher, Claude Berigard, and can be found on the twenty-fifth page of his *The Pisan Circle* <note>original: "Circulus Pisanus"</note>, published at Florence in 1641.

> “I did not think that it was possible to convert mercury <note>original: "quicksilver"</note> into gold, but an acquaintance thought proper to remove my doubt. He gave me about a dram <note>original: "drachm"</note> of a powder nearly the color of a wild poppy, and having a smell like burnt <note>original: "calcined"</note> sea-salt. To avoid all deception, I purchased a crucible, charcoal, and mercury, in which I was certain that there was no gold mixed. Ten drams of mercury which I heated on the fire were, upon projection <term>The act of casting the "philosopher's stone" or medicinal powder onto a molten base metal to effect its transformation.</term>, transmuted into nearly the same weight of good gold, which stood all tests. Had I not performed this operation in the most careful manner, taking every precaution against the possibility of doubt, I should not have believed it; but I am satisfied of the fact.”

## CHARNOCK.

Thomas Charnock was born on the Isle of Thanet in the year 1524. He calls himself an unlettered scholar and a student of astronomy and philosophy. He practiced surgery, and, though he knew only the basics of Latin,

<meta>This page continues the biography of Thomas Charnock, detailing how he acquired his alchemical knowledge from two specific masters.</meta>

### THOMAS CHARNOCK.

It appears that he was famous in the neighborhood of Salisbury, where he had established himself, for his accomplishments in the liberal sciences <note>The "liberal sciences" or "liberal arts" traditionally included grammar, logic, rhetoric, arithmetic, geometry, music, and astronomy.</note>. He had two masters in alchemy. The first was Sir James S——, a priest living <note>original: "dwelling"</note> in the cloisters near Salisbury, who informed Charnock that he did not derive his knowledge from any living expert <note>original: "adept"</note>. Instead, by meditating upon the words of the philosophers <note>In alchemical texts, "the philosophers" usually refers to the ancient and medieval authors of hermetic and chemical secrets.</note>, he had mastered the principal secrets of alchemy as he lay in his bed and had accordingly succeeded in making the silver powder.

The other master who instructed Charnock was a blind man, led by a boy, whom the student <note>original: "neophyte"</note> accidentally discovered at an inn among other travelers. Charnock recognized him by a few words of alchemical theory <note>original: "occult chemistry"</note> which he overheard in his conversation. As soon as the company had retired for the evening, Charnock questioned the speaker and requested instruction in natural philosophy. To this the expert <note>original: "adept"</note> objected, saying that he was unacquainted with his questioner. He said he would return his knowledge to God who gave it, unless he happened to meet a certain Master Charnock, the fame of whose learning and charity had reached him.

At these words, Charnock made himself known, and the old man talked with him for an hour, during which time he found Charnock expert in many mysteries of the sacred science. He promised Charnock that if he made a vow not to reveal the secret for gold, career advancement <note>original: "preferment"</note>, or through affection for powerful men, but only at death to one who was truly devoted to the search into nature, he would make him the heir of his knowledge. Accordingly, on the following Sunday they received Holy Communion <note>original: "the Eucharist"</note> together. Then, withdrawing into the middle of a large field, the boy was sent away out of hearing; there, in a few words, the blind man uttered “the mystery of mineral prudence” <term>The core secret of the "Great Work" or the transmutation of metals.</term>. Their conversations were continued for nine days.

<meta>This page continues the biographical account of the occultist John Dee and his partnership with the medium Edward Kelly, focusing on their alchemical discoveries and their departure from England for Central Europe.</meta>

# JOHN DEE

Whether he had been accused of forgery, or whether he had lost his ears or not <note>It was a common legal punishment for counterfeiters and forgers to have their ears cropped.</note>, the discovery of Edward Kelly caused the necromantic doctor <note>referring to John Dee, who was frequently accused of practicing "necromancy" or magic involving spirits</note> to be blind to Kelly's faults or his crimes. He immediately set to work in Kelly's company in the year 1579, and in December, a massive success crowned their shared labor. The potency of Kelly's tincture <note>an alchemical substance believed to be capable of transmuting base metals into gold</note> proved to be one part to 272,230; however, they lost much gold in experiments before they understood the full extent of its power. In Dr. Dee's "Diary in Germany," he mentions the book of St. Dunstan—which is likely Kelly's manuscript—and also the powder "found during the digging in England," which suggests there is some foundation for the story just told. The place where the treasure was obtained is reported to have been the ruins of Glastonbury Abbey, founded by St. Dunstan. The last abbot of the abbey was hanged by Henry VIII for his loyalty to the Papal cause.

Kelly appears to have lived at Islington. In June 1583, a warrant <note>original: "attachment"</note> was issued against him for counterfeiting money <note>original: "coining"</note>, an offense of which Dee declared him innocent. In the following September, Dr. Dee, his wife and children, and Edward Kelly with his wife—accompanied by a certain Lord Albert Alasco of Siradia, Poland—departed from London for Kraków. As soon as they arrived in northern Germany, Dr. Dee received a letter from one of his friends in England informing him that his library at Mortlake had been seized and partially destroyed due to common rumors <note>original: "vulgar report"</note> of his illegal studies, and that his rents and property were confiscated <note>original: "sequestered"</note>. Despite possessing the <term>Gift of God</term> <note>original: "Donum Dei," a term often used in alchemy to refer to the Philosopher's Stone or the secret of transmutation</note>, everyone involved appears to have lived in considerable poverty <note>original: "penury"</note> as a result.

In 1585, we find them at Prague, which was then the capital of alchemy and the headquarters for masters of the craft and their students.

<summary>This page details the alchemical collaboration between John Dee and Edward Kelly, their discovery of a "tincture" supposedly found at Glastonbury Abbey, and their flight from England to Poland and Prague following legal and social pressures.</summary>

### HENRY KHUNRATH.

This German alchemist, who is claimed as a high priest <term>original: "hierophant"; one who interprets sacred mysteries</term> of the spiritual <note>original: "psychic"</note> side of the Great Work <note>original: *magnum opus*</note>, and who was undoubtedly aware of the larger issues of Hermetic theories, must be classed as a follower of Paracelsus. He was a native of Saxony, born about the year 1560. He traveled throughout a large portion of Germany, and at the age of twenty-eight received the degree of medical doctor at the University of Basle. He practiced medicine at Hamburg and afterwards at Dresden, where he died in obscurity and poverty on the 9th of September 1601, aged about forty-five years. *The Amphitheater of the Only True Eternal Wisdom, Christian-Kabbalistic and Divinely Magical*, etc. <note>original: *Amphitheatrum Sapientiæ Æternæ solius veræ, Christiano Kabbalisticum divino magicum*</note>, published in large format <term>original: "folio"</term> in 1609, is the most curious and remarkable of his works, some of which still remain in manuscript.<margin>* Chausepié, *Dictionary*.</margin> It was left unfinished by its author, appearing four years after his death, with a preface and conclusion by his friend Erasmus Wohlfahrt.

The prologue directs the aspirant to the supreme temple of everlasting wisdom to know God and Jesus Christ whom He has sent, to know also himself, and the mysteries of the macrocosm <term>the universe as a whole, contrasted with the individual</term>. The whole treatise is purely mystical and magical. The seven steps leading to the portals of universal knowledge are described in an esoteric commentary on some portions of the Wisdom of Solomon. The Philosopher’s Stone <note>original: *lapis philosophorum*</note> is declared to be identical with the Spirit of God <note>original: *Ruach Elohim*</note> who hovered over the face of the waters during the first period of creation. The Spirit of God is called the "vapor of the power of God" <note>original: *vapor virtutis Dei*</note> and the internal form of all things. The perfect stone is attained through Christ, and, conversely, the possession of that treasure gives the...

### MICHAEL MAIER.

This celebrated German alchemist, one of the central figures of the Rosicrucian controversy in Germany and the greatest expert <note>original: "adept"</note> of his age, was born at Rendsburg <note>modern spelling of Ruidsburg</note>, in Holstein, around the year 1568. In his youth, he applied himself closely to the study of medicine. Establishing himself at Rostock, he practiced that art with so much success that he became physician to Emperor Rudolph II, who ennobled him for his services. 

Nevertheless, some experts <note>original: "adepts"</note> succeeded in enticing him away from the practical path he had followed for so long and into the thorny complexities <term>original: "tortuosities"</term> of alchemical labyrinths. He became passionate about the Great Work <note>original: *Il se passionna pour le grand œuvre*</note> and scoured all Germany to hold conferences with those whom he imagined to be in possession of transcendent secrets. The *Universal Biography* <note>original: *Biographie Universelle*</note> declares that he sacrificed his health, his fortune, and his time to these “ruinous absurdities.” According to Buhle<margin>* See De Quincey’s “Rosicrucians and Freemasons.”</margin>, he traveled extensively; on one occasion, he paid a visit to England, where he made the acquaintance of the Kentish mystic Robert Fludd.

He emerged as an alchemical writer shortly before the publication of the Rosicrucian manifestos. In the controversy that followed their appearance—which threw mystical Germany into turmoil—he took an early and enthusiastic part, defending the mysterious society in several books and pamphlets. He is supposed to have traveled in search of genuine members of the “College of Teutonic Philosophers R.C.” <note>R.C. stands for *Roseae Crucis*, or the Rosy Cross.</note> and, failing to meet with them, is said to have

It was not originally intended for publication, but manuscript copies were circulated by one of his friends, and as a result, he suffered much persecution from the church authorities of Görlitz. He was forbidden from writing any more books and was commanded to focus on his trade <note>Böhme was a shoemaker by trade</note>. For five years he humbly obeyed this tyrannical command, and afterward contented himself with writing only for his close friends. 

From 1619 to 1624, he produced a number of long treatises, of which the book of the *True Principles*, *The Great Mystery* <note>original: "Mysterium Magnum"</note>, and *The Signature of Things* <note>original: "Signatura Rerum"</note> are perhaps the most characteristic and important. The publication of his book *Way to Christ*—which was seemingly unauthorized <note>original: "surreptitious"</note>—again brought him into conflict with the religious authorities <note>original: "orthodoxy"</note> of Görlitz and led to his temporary exile. He was invited to the electoral court at Dresden, where a conference of famous theologians examined him. They were so greatly impressed by the man that they declared themselves unqualified <note>original: "incompetent"</note> to judge him.

In 1624, he fell ill with a fever at the house of a friend in Silesia. He was carried at his own request to his native town, and there, on the 22nd of November, he died in a state of spiritual ecstasy <note>original: "semi-ecstatic condition"</note>.

While serving his apprenticeship at Görlitz, Jacob Böhme acquired some knowledge of chemistry, and he subsequently used Hermetic <term>Hermetic: relating to alchemy and the secret occult sciences</term> terminology in a metaphorical and spiritual sense. His example was followed by his disciples, including the famous Louis Claude de Saint-Martin, Dionysius Andreas Freher, and William Law. The second writer mentioned, Freher, has discussed the similarities between the processes of the alchemical work <note>original: "philosophic work"</note> and the spiritual redemption of humanity through Jesus Christ, as explained by Jacob Böhme.

A treatise on metallurgy <term>metallurgy: the study of the properties of metals and their production and purification</term> is attributed to the theosophist <term>theosophist: someone who seeks a direct, mystical knowledge of God and the universe</term>...

## JEAN D’ESPAGNET.

This Hermetic philosopher is known to us by two treatises—*Handbook of Restored Physics* <note>original: *Enchiridion Physicæ Restitutæ*</note> and *The Secret of Hermetic Philosophy* <note>original: *Arcanum Philosophiæ Hermeticæ*</note>. This second work, however, has also been claimed as the production of an unknown individual who called himself the Imperial Knight <note>original: *Chevalier Impérial*</note>. “The Secret of Hermetic Philosophy” comprises the practical part of the Great Work <note>original: *magnum opus*</note>, and the *Handbook* contains the physical theory on which the possibility of transmutation is founded. D’Espagnet is also the author of the preface to the *Description of the Inconstancy of Demons* <note>original: *Tableau de l’Inconstance des Démons*</note>, by Pierre Delancre.

“The Secret of Hermetic Philosophy” is better known under the title of the “Canons of Espagnet,” and, as I have shown in the Introduction, it is claimed as a treatise on mystical alchemy. The author, however, very plainly states that “the science of producing Nature’s grand Secret is a perfect knowledge of Nature universally and of art, concerning the realm of metals; the practice of which involves finding the principles of metals by analysis.” Moreover, the authors whom Espagnet recommends as guides to the student include those who, like Bernard Trevisan <note>A 15th-century Italian alchemist who spent his life and fortune seeking the philosopher's stone</note>, are known to have spent their existence in practical alchemy. The Sethon-Sendivogius treatises <note>Works attributed to Alexander Sethon and Michael Sendivogius, famous for their claims of successful transmutation</note> are also respectfully cited. At the same time, it may be freely granted that much of the matter in the canons, though treating of a physical object, may be extended to the psychic side of the Hermetic art.

---

<note>Footnote in French</note>

* This knight, highly revered by alchemists, is mentioned often in the *French Trumpet* <note>original: *Trompette Française*</note>, a small volume containing a *Prophecy of Bombast <note>A reference to Paracelsus, whose full name was Philippus Aureolus Theophrastus Bombastus von Hohenheim</note> on the Birth of Louis XIV*. We have, by the Imperial Knight, the *Mirror of Alchemists* <note>original: *Miroir des Alchimistes*</note>, with instructions for ladies to be beautiful from now on without using their poisonous stings <note>original: *dards venimeux*; likely a metaphorical reference to toxic cosmetics</note>, 1609, 16mo. *Dictionary of Occult Sciences* <note>original: *Dictionnaire des Sciences Occultes*</note>.

<summary>This page introduces the works of Jean d'Espagnet, debating whether his writings focus on physical metallic transmutation or mystical, psychic alchemy.</summary>

->ALEXANDER SETHON.<-

### ALEXANDER SETHON.

Few alchemical adepts suffered more from the unwise display of their powers than the subject of this article. He was a native of Scotland and is believed to have lived in a mansion in a village near Edinburgh, close to the seashore.* In the summer of 1601, a Dutch vessel was wrecked on the coast, and some of the crew were saved through Sethon's assistance. He welcomed them into his home, treated them with great kindness, and provided them with the means to return to Holland. 

A year later, he visited James Haussen—the ship's pilot and one of the people he had rescued—at Enkhuysen in that country. The sailor received him with joy and hosted him for several weeks. During this time, Haussen watched with amazement as his guest performed several transmutations, eventually admitting that he was an alchemical adept. Although Haussen was bound by gratitude and friendship to maintain strict secrecy, he could not help sharing the marvel he had witnessed with Vanderlinden, a physician in Enkhuysen. Vanderlinden was a man of integrity and caution, and Sethon had even given him a piece of gold that had been transformed from lead in his presence on March 13, 1602. This curiosity eventually came into the possession of the doctor’s grandson, who showed it to the famous George Morhoff. Morhoff mentioned the gold and its history to Langlet du Fresnoy in a letter <note>original: "epistle"</note> concerning the transmutation of metals.

From Enkhuysen, Sethon proceeded to Amsterdam and—

<meta>This page continues the biography of Alexander Sethon, tracking his travels from the Netherlands through Italy, Switzerland, and Germany.</meta>

<page-num>172</page-num>

<header>LIVES OF ALCHEMICAL PHILOSOPHERS.</header>

Rotterdam, and then sailed for Italy. After a short stay there, he traveled into Switzerland and entered Germany. He was accompanied by Wolfgang Dienheim, an opponent of Hermetic philosophy <note>The tradition of alchemy and occult wisdom</note>, whom he convinced of his error through visible demonstration in the presence of several distinguished people in Basle.

To this opponent we are indebted for a description of Sethon, whom he described as looking very spiritual, short in stature but quite stout, with a high-colored complexion and a French-style beard. He calls him Alexander Sethonius and states that he was a native of Molier, "on an island in the ocean."

The lead required for the transmutation was brought by Jacob Zwinger from his own house; a crucible was borrowed from a goldsmith, and common sulfur was purchased on the road to the house where the operation was to be performed. During the entire experiment, Sethon touched nothing. He simply provided the small packet containing the powder of projection <note>A substance believed to trigger the transformation of base metal into gold</note>, which transformed the base metal into gold of the purest quality, equal in weight to the original lead.

The experiment was repeated on another occasion with the same brilliant success. In addition to Dienheim’s testimony, we also have that of Zwinger, a name highly respected by Germans in the history of medicine.*

Alexander Sethon left Basle and went under an assumed name to Strasbourg, from where he proceeded to Cologne. He stayed with an amateur alchemist named Anton Bordemann, who introduced him to the other <term>souffleurs</term> <note>literally "puffers," a derogatory term for amateur alchemists who focused on the physical labor of the bellows rather than the philosophy</note> of that city. He began a kind of alchemical crusade among them, recklessly exposing his knowledge to the gullible and suspi—

<meta>This page continues the biography of Alexander Sethon, following his journey from Cologne to Hamburg, Munich, and finally to the court of the Elector of Saxony.</meta>

<header>ALEXANDER SETHON.</header>

...identical alike, and producing on one occasion six ounces of the precious metal by means of a single grain of his great philosophical tincture.*

Leaving Cologne completely stunned by his marvelous operations, the illustrious <term>hierophant</term> <note>a high priest or person who explains sacred mysteries</note> of the <term>Hermetic</term> <note>the secret or occult tradition of alchemy</note> art traveled to Hamburg, where his further amazing <term>projections</term> <note>the process of turning base metal into gold</note> are described by George Morhoff. At Munich, the next stop in his alchemical pilgrimage, he performed no transmutations, suddenly disappearing with the daughter of one of its citizens. He appears to have legally married her, and he remained most devotedly attached to her from then on.

The fame of Sethon around this time attracted the attention of Christian II, the young Elector of Saxony. He sent for the alchemist, but Sethon—absorbed by his passion—had let the lover replace the <term>Hermetic</term> promoter. He instead sent William Hamilton, who appeared to be his servant but was in reality a confidant and friend, to convince the Elector of the truth of <term>alchemical</term> operations through eyewitness evidence. A <term>projection</term> was performed by Hamilton with perfect success in the presence of the whole court, and the gold manufactured there resisted every test.†

The Elector, previously a skeptic, was now more eager than ever to meet the adept. Sethon reluctantly consented, and at this point, he seems to have been left by Hamilton. He was received with honor and favor, and he presented a small quantity of the powder to Christian II. However, the Elector soon tried to take the philosopher’s entire secret for himself. Sethon refused to satisfy him and was deaf to both persuasion and threats; but the Elector, convinced that he was in possession of a

† Galdenfalk, “Alchemical Anecdotes.”

> “You see what I have endured,” he continued, “my nerves are withered <note>original: "shrunk"</note>, my limbs are dislocated; I am starved to the point of death, and my body is almost decaying; even to avoid all this, I did not reveal the secrets of alchemy <note>original: "philosophy"; in this context, the "secrets of philosophy" refers specifically to Hermetic philosophy or the secret of transmuting metals</note>.”

Sendivogius was not, however, destined to be deprived of all reward for his efforts and self-sacrifice. Alexander Sethon did not enjoy his freedom for long after his friend had obtained it for him, and upon his death, which occurred two years after his escape, he gave his rescuer the remains of his transmuting powder.

### MICHAEL SENDIVOGIUS.

Sendivogius, whose true name was Sensophax, was born in Moravia in 1566, and was therefore about thirty-eight years old at the death of his silent <note>original: "taciturn"</note> master. He is said by some of his biographers to have been the illegitimate <note>original: "natural"</note> son of a Polish nobleman named Jacob Sendimir. His life has been written at some length by his supporter, an anonymous German who, however, produced a work of fiction rather than a history; among other fantasies, he represented his hero as being sent by the Emperor Rudolf II to the East, where he received the discovery of the great secret from a Greek patriarch. In reality, Sendivogius had made no progress in <term>alchemy</term> <note>alchemy: a medieval chemical science and speculative philosophy aiming to achieve the transmutation of base metals into gold and the discovery of a universal cure for disease</note> before his acquaintance with Sethon.

Having almost exhausted his fortune to obtain the liberation of that adept, and having a taste for a lavish lifestyle <note>original: "extravagant living"</note>, he was unsatisfied with simply owning a portion of the transmuting powder, and was more eager...

--- Page 185 ---
<meta>This page continues a discussion on the philosophical foundations of alchemy before transitioning into a specific historical anecdote about an alchemist named Gustenhover.</meta>

organic and inorganic development they beheld as a Promised Land.

The "New Light of Alchemy" <note>Original title: *Novum Lumen Chymicum* (1604), a highly influential work attributed to Michael Sendivogius.</note> insists on the existence of a seed <note>original: "sperm," used here in the archaic sense of a vital reproductive principle found in all matter</note> in everything, and that all Nature originated at the beginning from one single seed. It treats the generation of metals and the manner of their differentiation, the extraction of their seed, and the manufacture of the "stone" or tincture <note>The "Philosopher's Stone" or "Tincture" was the legendary substance believed to be capable of curing disease and transmuting base metals into gold.</note>.

### GUSTENHOVER.

A respectable goldsmith named Gustenhover resided at Strasburg <note>Modern-day Strasbourg, France.</note> in 1603. In a time of great peril, he gave shelter to a certain M. Hirschborgen, who is described as a good and religious man. On leaving his house after a considerable stay, this person presented his humane host with some "powder of projection" <term>a concentrated alchemical substance used to trigger the transformation of base metal into gold</term> and then, departing on his journey, was heard of no more.

Gustenhover imprudently performed transmutations before numerous people, and the fact was reported to the Emperor Rudolph II, who was himself an amateur in alchemy. He wrote to the magistrates of Strasburg, directing that the goldsmith should be immediately sent to him. The order was zealously obeyed; the man was arrested and guarded with vigilance to prevent any possibility of escape. When he discovered that the intention of his imprisonment was to send him to the Emperor at Prague, he guessed the whole of the business. He invited the magistrates to meet together, asking them to bring a crucible <note>original: "crucet"</note> and charcoal, and—without approaching it himself—asked them to melt some lead. Musket balls were used for the purpose, and when the metal was molten, he handed them a small portion of red powder. They cast this into the crucible, and the result of their <term>calcination</term> <note>The process of heating a substance to high temperatures in the presence of air.</note> was a considerable quantity of pure gold.

I had many questions to ask him which left me in doubt. I returned to work according to the recipe <note>original: "receipt"</note>, but failed to get the result. I repeated the process with more care; it was all in vain! Yet I persevered until I had spent all the transmuted gold and the greater part of my own property."

--- Page 193 ---
<meta>This page continues the biographical discussion of Eirenæus Philalethes, focusing on the debate regarding his identity and birth year compared to Eugenius Philalethes (Thomas Vaughan).</meta>

### EIRENÆUS PHILALETHES

The author was consequently born in the year 1621, the same year that Eugenius Philalethes—also known as Thomas Vaughan—first saw the light <note>was born</note>. This would remain undisputed, except for the fact that the original edition* of the *Introitus* <note>Full title: *Introitus Apertus ad Occlusum Regis Palatium* or "The Open Entrance to the Closed Palace of the King," a famous alchemical text</note> is claimed to read "in the thirtieth year" <note>original Latin: *trigesimo anno*</note>—interpreted as the thirty-third year—instead of "in the twentieth year" <note>original Latin: *vigesimo anno*</note>. There is no copy of this original edition in the British Museum, and my knowledge of it comes from the reprint in Langlet du Fresnoy’s *History of Hermetic Philosophy* <note>original title: *Histoire de la Philosophie Hermétique*</note>. Eirenæus, according to later printings, is honored by the followers of Hermes <note>alchemists and students of Hermeticism</note> as the adept who achieved the "grand and sublime act" <note>The successful creation of the Philosopher's Stone, the goal of alchemy</note> at the age of twenty-two.

These reasons, which are significant in themselves, may be supported by the fact that there is a great deal of similarity in the writing styles and methods of the two authors.

Eugenius Philalethes wrote *Theomagical Anthroposophy* <note>original: *Anthroposophia Theomagica*</note> and *The Hidden Magical Soul* <note>original: *Anima Magica Abscondita*</note>, both published together in 1650; *Adamic Magic* <note>original: *Magia Adamica*</note>, 1650; "The Man-Mouse" (a satire on Henry More, the Platonist); "The Second Wash, or The Moore (meaning Henry More) Scoured once more," 1651; *Light from Light* <note>original: *Lumen de Lumine*</note>, 1651; "The Fame and Confession of the Fraternity of the Rosy Cross" <note>original: *The Fame and Confession of the Fraternity R.C.*, referring to the Rosicrucians</note>, 1652; *The Hall of Light* <note>original: *Aula Lucis*</note>, 1652; and "Euphrates, or The Waters of the East," 1655. "A Brief Natural History," published in 1669, also bears his name, and in 1679 his surviving poems were published by his brother, Henry, along with some of Henry's own work, titled *Thalia Reborn* <note>original: *Thalia Rediviva*</note>.

The level of confusion existing among biographers and bibliographers on this subject can be seen in the fact that some authorities claim Thomas Vaughan died in 1656, while Chalmers’ *Biographical Dictionary* attributes all the works of Eugenius Philalethes to Henry "the Silurist" <note>Henry Vaughan, a famous poet known as "the Silurist" because he lived in the ancient territory of the Silures tribe in Wales</note>, whom Chalmers describes as a "Rosicrucian fanatic."

---

\* It was published at Amsterdam in 1667 and is supposed to have been free from the numerous printing errors of the later editions.

<meta>This page continues the author's explanation of why he writes in a plain style and the personal hardships he faces as an alchemist who has achieved the "Great Work."</meta>

-> ### EIRENÆUS PHILALETHES. <-

...sophistical operations. I have not written in that style, having surrendered my will to God's pleasure. I do not fear that the art <note>The "art" refers to alchemy, specifically the creation of the Philosopher's Stone.</note> will be looked down upon because I write plainly, for true wisdom will defend its own honor. I wish gold and silver were as lowly in value as common soil, so that we would not need to hide ourselves so strictly. For we are like Cain, driven from the pleasant society we once enjoyed without fear; now we are tossed back and forth as if pursued by furies <note>original: "furies" — in mythology, these are female spirits of vengeance; here, they represent constant anxiety and pursuit.</note>; nor can we feel safe in any one place for long. We weep and sigh, complaining to the Lord,

> "Behold, whoever finds me will kill me!" <note>The author is quoting Genesis 4:14, comparing his life of hiding to that of the biblical Cain.</note>

We travel through many nations like wanderers, and do not dare to take on the responsibility of a family; nor do we have any permanent home. Although we possess all things <note>As an adept, he claims the ability to create infinite wealth, yet cannot use it.</note>, we can use only a few. What, therefore, do we enjoy except the thoughts of our minds? Many who are strangers to this art imagine that if they possessed it, they would do great good. I once believed this myself, but the danger I have experienced has taught me otherwise. Anyone who faces the imminent danger of losing their life will act with more caution from then on. I found the world in a most wicked state; there is hardly a man who is not guided by some selfish and unworthy motive, regardless of how honest or upright he appears in public. An adept <term>adept: a person who has successfully mastered the secrets of alchemy and created the Philosopher's Stone</term> cannot perform works of mercy on a large scale without trusting others to keep his secret, and this puts him at risk of imprisonment and death. I recently had proof of this; for, while in a foreign country, I administered the medicine to some distressed poor people who were dying. Because they recovered miraculously, a rumor immediately spread about the elixir of life, so much so that I was forced to flee by night with great difficulty, having changed my clothes, shaved my head, put on a wig <note>original: "other hair"</note>, and changed my name.

--- Page 196 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative of Eirenaeus Philalethes, who is describing the extreme personal dangers and legal risks faced by an "adept"—someone who has successfully discovered the secret of alchemy.</meta>

-> ### LIVES OF ALCHEMISTICAL PHILOSOPHERS. <-

...lay in wait for me, merely on suspicion, driven by a thirst for gold. I could mention other dangers that might seem ridiculous to those who have never been in a similar situation. These people think they would manage their affairs better, but they do not realize that the intelligent people whose company is most sought after are extremely perceptive; a mere hint of suspicion is enough to spark a conspiracy. For the wickedness of men is so great that I have known a person who was strangled with a rope <note>original: "strangled with a halter"</note> based only on suspicion. Although he did not actually possess the art <note>The "art" refers to the secret of alchemy and the creation of the Philosopher's Stone.</note>, it was enough that a desperate man had heard reports of it. 

This age is full of ignorant alchemists; however little they know of science, they know enough to recognize or suspect a true adept <note>An "adept" is a person who has mastered the Great Work of alchemy.</note>. Any appearance of secrecy will cause them to investigate every detail of your life. If you heal the sick or sell a large amount of gold, the news spreads throughout the entire neighborhood. The goldsmith knows when the metal is too pure, and it is illegal for anyone who is not a regular metallurgist to mix it with alloys. 

I once sold pure silver worth £600 in a foreign country. Even though I was dressed as a merchant, the goldsmith told me, "This silver was created by art." I asked him why he said that. He replied, "I know the silver that comes from Spain, England, and so on. This is purer than any of them." Upon hearing this, I left immediately. There is no better silver in the trade than Spanish silver, but if I had tried to lower the purity of my silver <note>The author means adding base metals to the pure silver to make it look like common "legal" silver.</note> and was discovered, I would have been hanged for a felony <note>Tampering with precious metals or unofficial alloying was often a capital crime.</note>. I never went back for either the silver or the payment for it. The transport of gold and silver from one country to another is governed by strict laws, and this alone is enough to condemn an adept who is found with a large quantity of it. Therefore, having been taught by these hardships, I have determined to remain hidden, and I will communicate the art to you, who dream of

> "The Searcher of all hearts knows that I write the truth; nor is there any reason to accuse me of envy <note>original: "envy" – here meaning a begrudging or secretive attitude regarding his knowledge</note>. The thing we have seen, taught, and made—which we have, possess, and know—that is what we declare. 
> 
> We are moved with compassion for the studious, and with indignation toward gold, silver, and precious stones—not as they are creatures of God (far be it from us, for in that respect we honor them and think them worthy of esteem), but because the people of God adore them just as the world does. Therefore, let them be ground to powder like the golden calf <note>A reference to Exodus 32, where Moses destroyed the idol worshipped by the Israelites.</note>! 
> 
> I hope and expect that within a few years, money will be as worthless as dross <note>original: "dross" – the scum or waste matter produced when smelting metals</note>; and that prop of the anti-Christian beast <note>A reference to the apocalyptic "Beast" from the Book of Revelation; the author associates the global monetary system with its power.</note> will be dashed to pieces. The people are mad, the nations rave; a worthless being <note>original: "unprofitable wight"</note> is set up in the place of God. At our long-expected and approaching redemption, the New Jerusalem shall abound with gold in the streets, its gates shall be made of solid stones—most precious ones—and the Tree of Life in the midst of Paradise shall give leaves for the *healing* of...

--- Page 198 ---
<meta>This page continues the writings of Eirenaeus Philalethes, an anonymous alchemist, as he discusses the spiritual and social implications of alchemical knowledge and lists the authors who influenced his work.</meta>

...the nations. I know these my writings will be to men as pure gold; and through them gold and silver will become as worthless as dirt. Believe me, the time is at the door—I see it in spirit—when we, the adepts <term>adepts</term> <note>original: "adeptists" — those who have achieved the "Great Work" of alchemy.</note>, shall return from the four corners of the earth. We shall not fear any traps that are laid against our lives, but we shall give thanks to the Lord our God. I wish to God that every clever man in the whole earth understood this science; then it would only be valued for its wisdom, and only virtue would be held in honor. I know many adepts who have vowed a most secret silence. I am of another judgment because of the hope I have in my God; therefore, I did not consult with my brothers, or with any human authority <note>original: "flesh and blood"</note>, in these my writings: God grant that it be to the glory of His name!”

We are told in the preface to *Ripley Revived* <note>A famous commentary by Philalethes on the works of the 15th-century English alchemist George Ripley.</note> which authors he was, at any rate, chiefly indebted to.

> “For my own part, I have cause to honor Bernard Trévisan <note>A 15th-century Italian nobleman and alchemist.</note>, who is very clever, especially in his letter to Thomas of Boulogne, where I sincerely confess I received the main light regarding the hidden secret. I do not remember that I ever learned anything from Raymond Lully <note>A 13th-century philosopher and alchemist from Majorca.</note>. Some who are not adepts give more instruction to a beginner than someone whom perfect knowledge makes cautious. I learned the secret of the magnet from one, the *steel* <note>original: *chalybs* — often referring to a specific preparation of antimony or iron used as a catalyst.</note> from another, the use of *Diana’s Doves* <note>An alchemical symbol for the white, purified vapors of mercury.</note> from a third, the *air* or *chameleon* <note>Refers to the changing colors or the volatile state of the matter during the process.</note> from another, the rough preparation of the solvent from another, and the number of 'eagles' <note>In alchemy, "eagles" refers to the number of times a substance has been distilled or sublimated.</note> from another; but for operations on the *true matter* and signs of the *true mercury*, I know of none like Ripley, though Flamel <note>Nicolas Flamel (c. 1330–1418), a famous French scribe and reputed alchemist.</note> is also eminent. I know what I say, having learned by experience what is truth and what is error.
>

->### EIRENÆUS PHILALETHES.<-

> "...and having at last, through the undeserved mercy of God, arrived at my haven of rest, I shall reach out my hand to those who follow. I have written several treatises: one in English, which is very clear but unfinished—unfortunately, it was lost. I would be sorry if it were published to the world. I also wrote two in Latin, *A Brief Guide to the Celestial Ruby* <note>original: *Brevis Manuductio ad Rubinem Cœlestem*</note> and *The Fountain of Chemical Philosophy* <note>original: *Fons Chymicæ Philosophiæ*</note>—these, for specific reasons, I have decided to suppress. Two others I wrote recently, which you may perhaps enjoy: namely, *The Art of the Metamorphosis of Metals* <note>original: *Ars Metallorum Metamorphoses*</note> and *An Open Entrance to the Closed Palace of the King* <note>original: *Introitus Apertus ad Occlusum Regis Palatium*</note>. I wrote two poems in English which are lost; also in English, a manual <note>original: "Enchiridion"</note> of experiments, a daily journal of meditations, and many recipes explaining the entire secret, with an enigma attached. These also fell into the hands of one who, I believe, will never return them."

The offender in question was undoubtedly <term>George Starkey</term> <note>A colonial American alchemist and physician who moved to London; he is widely believed by modern scholars to be the actual author behind the Philalethes persona.</note>, who published the "Marrow of Alchemy" under the name of Eirenæus Philoponos Philalethes. This poetic account of the <term>Hermetic theory</term> <note>The philosophical and practical system of alchemy, named after Hermes Trismegistus.</note> and practice is apparently the lost verse of the <term>adept</term> <note>One who has mastered the "Great Work" of alchemy.</note>, but it also contains an account of the editor’s own initiation, which is certainly worth transcribing:

> "I must now state, from my own experience, facts of <term>transmutation</term> <note>The alchemical process of changing base metals into gold or silver.</note> of which I was an eye-witness. I was well-acquainted with an alchemical practitioner <term>artist</term> <note>In this context, "artist" refers specifically to a master of the alchemical art.</note> with whom I often spoke on the subject, and I saw in his possession the white and the red <term>elixir</term> <note>The substances (also called the Philosopher's Stone) used to perform transmutation; the white converts metal to silver, and the red to gold.</note> in very large quantities. He gave me more than two ounces of the white medicine, which was powerful enough to convert 120,000 times its weight into the purest pure silver. With this treasure, I went to work ignorantly on <term>multiplication</term> <note>The process of increasing the quantity or potency of the alchemical elixir.</note> and was caught in the trap of my own greed, for I spent or wasted all of this <term>tincture</term> <note>Another name for the elixir or "medicine."</note>. However, I performed a <term>projection</term> <note>The final stage of the alchemical process: casting the elixir onto molten base metal to transform it.</note> with part of it, which is sufficient..."

...for my present purpose, enabling me to assert the possibility of the art through eye-witness demonstration. I have many times transformed <note>original: "tinged"</note> hundreds of ounces into the finest silver. From a pound of mercury, I have produced silver within less than a scruple of its original weight; from lead, there was very little more waste. But it is wondrous to see tin—even though impurities <note>original: "dross"</note> were burned away from it, its weight actually increased in the fire. I tested the medicine <note>In alchemy, "the medicine" refers to the Philosopher's Stone or the substance used for transmutation.</note> on copper, iron, even on brass and pewter, on <term>spelter</term> <note>an old term for zinc</note>, solder, <term>tinglass</term> <note>an archaic name for bismuth</note>, mercury, and on <term>regulus of antimony</term> <note>the metallic form of antimony</note>; and I can say with truth that it conquers all metallic things and brings them all to perfection. I found there was nothing related to metals that it would not transform into pure silver.

Even perfect gold was penetrated and changed into a white glass which would <term>transmute</term> <note>transform one element into another</note> inferior metals into silver, though only in small quantities. But when this silver was tested, it was found to withstand <term>*aquafortis*</term> <note>original: *aqua fortis*; literally "strong water," or nitric acid, used to test and separate metals</note> and the cupel of antimony, and it weighed as much as gold, so that it was **white gold**. This was because the white <term>tincture</term> <note>the alchemical agent of transformation</note> had fermented with <term>red earth</term> <note>likely referring to the final red stage of the alchemical process or the "Red Stone"</note>, and both powers combining during <term>projection</term> <note>the final stage of alchemy where the "medicine" is cast upon base metal to transform it</note> produced silver-colored gold—or silver equaling gold in perfection, but lacking its color. I did not know the value of this silver until my medicine was nearly gone; I sold eighty ounces of it at the regular price for silver, even though it was as valuable as gold. I cast the medicine upon pure silver and obtained a crystalline metal like burnished steel or a mirror, but there was no increase in its power; it transformed only as much as it would have if it had not been cast upon silver.

The artist who gave me this is still living; I prize him as my own life and wish for his happiness, for he has been a steadfast friend. He is currently on his travels, visiting fellow artists and collecting antiquities as a citizen of the world. He is an Englishman from an ancient, honorable family who still live in the place where he was born. He is barely thirty-three years old and is exceptionally <note>original: "rarely"</note> learned. > "He told me this mercury was a treasure beyond compare if God would open my eyes to its use; otherwise, I might grope in the dark. With this <term>dissolvent</term> <note>a substance used to dissolve others; in alchemy, often a universal solvent</note>, which is the hidden secret of all masters, he immensely increased the power of his red stone. I saw him put a measured piece of the red stone into that same mercury; it then <term>digested</term> <note>a process of slow, steady heating in a sealed vessel</note> and dissolved it, causing it to change color. I then began to reflect on the principles found in the old books, considering how my work agreed with the laws of Nature. At last, I concluded that each thing must be treated according to its own nature.

> "When I realized that my vain attempts were only wasting the <term>tincture</term> <note>the concentrated alchemical substance used for transmutation</note>, I stopped, resolving to save the few remaining grains for some urgent need. To preserve it, I mixed it with ten parts of silver.
