### EIRENAEUS PHILALETHES.

...the evil he does is blamed on the master <note>original: "adept"</note> who was so unwise. Consider what a prize you possessed in both the *stone* and the *mercury*. Would anyone not say that a person must be mad to throw it all away without any gain?

"If you had been guided by reason, you might still have enough of what I gave you. Your method was to add only a grain of the *stone* to the purest *gold*; once melted <note>original: "in fusion"</note>, they would unite. Then you could proceed with your *mercury*, which would quickly mix with that gold and greatly shorten the process. You could easily have managed the work until it reached the *red* <note>original: "red"; refers to the Rubedo, the final stage of the alchemical Great Work</note> stage. Since you saw how I joined new *gold* to such *sulfur* and *mercury*, and you observed the weight, time, and heat required, what more could you have wanted? And since you know the art of preparing the *fiery mercury*, you could have as much in stock as anyone else.

"But you do not realize that God is displeased with you and caused you to waste the treasure I gave you. Perhaps He sees that you would break His holy laws and use it wrongly. Although He has granted you so much knowledge, I see clearly that He will keep you for several years from enjoying what you would undoubtedly misuse. Know that if you attempt this art without a <term>ferment</term> <note>a substance added to the alchemical mixture to trigger transformation, similar to how yeast works in bread</note>, you must beware of constant mistakes. You will wander from the right path despite all your care, and you might never in your lifetime obtain this treasure, which is a gift from God alone. If you follow the most direct path, it will take a year to reach perfection. But if you take the wrong turns, you will often be delayed—sometimes for a whole year—and you must restart your efforts and labor, regretting your loss and errors amidst much distraction, worry, and danger, all at a cost you can hardly afford. Listen, therefore, to my advice, and I will disclose the secret to you on one condition. Swear before the mighty God that for a certain time you will abstain from attempting or practic—

<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Eirenaeus Philalethes and his mentor, concluding with the mentor's demand for secrecy, and then transitions into an analysis of Philalethes’ works and an introduction to the scholar Pierre Jean Fabre.</meta>

> “I swore, and he shared his mind with me, proving he was not deceiving me by showing me those truths <note>original: "lights"</note> which I shall honestly recount, as far as my oath allows.”

Eirenaeus Philalethes is credited with exceptional clarity <note>original: "unexampled perspicuity"</note>, and his *Open Entrance* <note>original: *Introitus Apertus*</note>, in particular, is an abridgment or summary of the entire *Assembly of Philosophers* <note>original: *turba philosophorum*</note>. Those who are searching for the physical secret of alchemy should begin by carefully studying his works. From there, they should move on to the authors he recommends; after which, the best Hermetic writers—from the days of <term>Geber</term> <note>an influential 8th-century chemist and alchemist, often called the father of chemistry</note> onward—should be read in their chronological order, carefully analyzed, and their similarities and differences noted.

The physical nature of the alchemical <term>secrets</term> <note>original: "arcana"</note> held by the true Philalethes are best understood through the writings and commentaries of his pupil, <term>George Starkey</term> <note>an American-born physician and alchemist (1628–1665) who moved to London and claimed to have met a true master of the art</note>. The mystery surrounding such an <term>adept</term> <note>a person who has mastered the "Great Work" of alchemy</note> excites overactive imaginations and makes his projects and supposed achievements seem legendary <note>original: "Titanic stature"</note>. Although the *Open Entrance* contains much that is mystical and open to romantic interpretation, it is ultimately a manual of practical alchemy. It further develops the physical principles explained in the verse essays preserved and published by Starkey.

This physician from Montpellier, who contributed to the progress of chemistry, lived during the...

beginning of the seventeenth century. He believed in the transmutation of metals, but is not considered a master <note>original: "adept"</note>, though he wrote seventeen treatises on this subject and on <term>Spagyric Medicine</term> <note>a branch of medicine based on alchemical principles, specifically the separation and recombination of plant or mineral components</note>. His most curious work is *The Christian Alchemist* <note>original: *Alchimista Christianus*</note> (Toulouse, 1632, octavo). In *The Pious-Chemical Hercules* <note>original: *Hercules Piochymicus*</note>, published in the same place two years later, he maintains that the labors of Hercules are allegories which contain the secrets <note>original: "arcana"</note> of <term>Hermetic philosophy</term> <note>the ancient philosophical and spiritual system focused on the relationship between the divine and the material world through alchemy</note>.

He defines the philosophical stone as the seed out of which gold and silver are generated. It is three and yet one; it may be found in all compounded substances and is formed of salt, mercury, and sulphur. These, however, are not to be confused with the common <note>original: "vulgar"</note> substances of the same names.

## HELVETIUS.

The following singularly impressive and even convincing testimony to the reported fact of metallic transmutation was published by the eminent Dutch physician, John Frederick Helvetius, at the Hague in 1667. It was dedicated to his friends, Dr. Retius of Amsterdam, Dr. Hansius of Heidelberg, and Dr. Menzelin of Brandenburg.

> “On the 27th of December 1666, in the afternoon, a stranger in plain, rural dress came to my house at the Hague. His manner of speaking was honest, serious, and authoritative; he was short in stature, with a long face, black hair, and a smooth-shaven chin. He seemed like a native of the north of Scotland, and I guessed he was about forty-four years old. After greeting me, he requested most respectfully that I pardon his unannounced intrusion, explaining that his love of the <term>chemical arts</term> <note>original: "pyrotechnic art"</note> led him to visit me. Having read some of my short treatises, particularly the one written against the <term>sympathetic powder</term> <note>original: "sympathetic powder of Sir Kenelm Digby"; a contemporary medicinal theory that a wound could be healed by applying a powder to the weapon that caused it</note> of Sir Kenelm Digby, and having observed in them my...”

Because he had some doubt about the Hermetic mystery <note>the secrets of alchemy and divine transformation</note>, it caused him to request this interview. He asked me if I still believed that there was no medicine in Nature capable of curing all diseases, unless the vital organs—such as the lungs or liver—had failed, or the time of death had come. I replied that I had never met an adept <note>original: "adept," a master who has successfully created the philosophers' stone</note> or seen such a medicine, though I had read much about it and often wished for it.

Then I asked him if he was a physician. He replied that he was a brass founder, yet from his youth he had learned many rare things in chemistry—specifically from a friend—including the method of extracting many medicinal secrets <note>original: "arcana"</note> from metals through the use of fire. After we discussed experiments with metals, he asked me, "Would you recognize the philosophers’ stone if you saw it?"

I answered that I would not; though I had read much about it in the works of Paracelsus, Helmont, Basil <note>Basil Valentine, a legendary 15th-century alchemist</note>, and others, I dared not say I could recognize the substance of the philosophers. Meanwhile, he pulled a polished ivory box from his breast pocket and took out three heavy lumps of the stone, each about the size of a small walnut. They were transparent and of a pale sulfur <note>original: "brimstone"</note> color; some scales from the crucible still stuck to them from when this most noble substance was melted. I later calculated that its value was equal to twenty tons of gold. 

When I had eagerly examined and handled the stone for nearly fifteen minutes, and heard from its owner many rare secrets regarding its wonderful effects on human and metallic bodies, as well as its other amazing properties, I returned this "treasure of treasures" to him. I did so with a truly sorrowful heart, like one struggling with self-denial, yet I was also very thankful and humble, as was proper. I further asked to know why the color was yellow, and not red, ruby-colored, or purple, as the alchemical authors describe. He answered that the color did not matter, for the substance was mature and ripe enough. Then I humbly asked him to give me a small piece of the medicine as a lasting memory of him, even if it were only...

### JOHN FREDERICK HELVETIUS.

...gravely asserting that it was only to magnify the sweet fame and mercy of the most glorious God; that few men endeavored to serve Him, and he expressed this like a pastor or minister of a church. But I recalled his attention, entreating him to show me the <term>metallic mystery</term> <note>original: "metallic mystery"; the secret art of alchemy and the creation of the Philosopher's Stone.</note>, also desiring that he would eat, drink, and stay at my house. I pressed him on this, but he was of such a fixed determination that all my efforts were frustrated. I could not help telling him that I had a laboratory ready for an experiment, and that a promised favor was a kind of debt. "Yes, true," said he, "but I promised to teach you upon my return, with this condition: if it is not forbidden."

When I perceived that all of this was in vain, I earnestly requested a small crumb of his <term>powder</term> <note>The "projection powder," a powdered form of the Philosopher's Stone used to trigger transmutation.</note>, sufficient to <term>transmute</term> <note>To change the nature of a substance; in alchemy, specifically turning "base" metals like lead into "noble" metals like gold.</note> a few grains of lead to gold. At last, out of his <term>philosophical commiseration</term> <note>original: "philosophical commiseration"; the stranger's pity or compassion as a fellow seeker of wisdom.</note>, he gave me a piece as small as a turnip seed, saying, "Receive this small portion of the greatest treasure in the world, which truly few kings or princes have ever seen or known." "But," I said, "this perhaps will not transmute even four grains of lead," whereupon he told me to give it back to him. In hopes of receiving a larger portion, I did so; but he, cutting half of it off with his fingernail, threw it into the fire and gave me the rest wrapped neatly in blue paper, saying, "It is still sufficient for you." 

I answered him, indeed with a very dejected face, "Sir, what does this mean? The other portion being too small, you now give me even less." He told me to put half an ounce of lead into the <term>crucible</term> <note>A ceramic or metal container used for melting materials at very high temperatures.</note>, for no more lead should be added than the <term>medicine</term> <note>original: "medicine"; alchemists often referred to the Stone as a "medicine" that "healed" or "purified" imperfect metals into gold.</note> can transmute. I gave him great thanks for my diminished treasure, which was truly concentrated to the highest degree, and put it carefully away into my little box, saying I intended to try it the next day and would not reveal it to anyone. "Not so, not so," said he, "for we ought to reveal all things to the <term>children of art</term> <note>original: "children of art"; a traditional term for fellow practitioners and students of alchemy.</note> which may lead..."

"...alone for the honor of God, so that they may live in the theosophical truth." I then made a confession to him: that while the mass of his <term>medicine</term> <note>In alchemy, the "Medicine" refers to the Philosopher's Stone or the elixir used to "heal" base metals into gold.</note> was in my hands, I had attempted to scrape away a little of it with my nail and could not resist; but I scratched off so very little that, after it was picked from my nail and wrapped in paper, I cast it onto melted lead. I found no <term>transmutation</term> <note>The alchemical process of changing one element into another, specifically lead into gold.</note>, but almost the whole mass of lead <term>sublimed</term> <note>original: "sublimed"; vaporized or turned into smoke by the heat.</note>, while the remainder was merely a <term>glassy earth</term> <note>A brittle, slag-like residue left over from an unsuccessful chemical reaction.</note>. 

At this unexpected account, he immediately said, "You are more skillful at committing theft than at applying the medicine; for if you had only wrapped the stolen prey in yellow wax to protect it from the fumes of the lead, it would have sunk to the bottom and transmuted the lead to gold. But because you cast it directly into the fumes, the violence of the vapor—partly through its <term>sympathetic alliance</term> <note>The alchemical belief that substances with similar "spiritual" natures are naturally drawn to one another.</note>—carried the medicine entirely away." I brought him the <term>crucible</term> <note>A ceramic or metal container used for melting substances at very high temperatures.</note>, and he saw a most beautiful saffron-colored <term>tincture</term> <note>A stain or residue left by the alchemical substance.</note> sticking to the sides. He promised to come the next morning at nine o'clock to show me that this tincture would transmute the lead into gold. 

Having taken his leave, I impatiently awaited his return, but the next day he did not come, nor has he ever since. He sent an excuse at half-past nine that morning and promised to come at three in the afternoon, but I never heard from him again. I soon began to doubt the whole matter. Late 그 night my wife, who was a most curious student and inquirer into the art, came to me, begging me to make an experiment with that little grain of the stone we had been given, just to be sure of the truth. "Unless this is done," she said, "I shall have no rest or sleep tonight." 

Since she was so earnest, I ordered a fire to be made, saying to myself, "I fear, I fear indeed, that this man has deceived me." My wife wrapped the small piece of matter in wax, and I cut half an ounce of lead and put it into a crucible in the fire. Once it was melted, my wife put in the medicine, which had been made into a small...

->## JOHN FREDERICK HELVETIUS.<-

I wrapped the small pill in wax, which immediately made a hissing noise. In a quarter of an hour, the mass of lead was totally <term>transmuted</term> <note>The alchemical process of changing one element into another, specifically lead into gold.</note> into the best and finest gold, which amazed us exceedingly. We could not gaze enough upon this admirable and miraculous work of nature; for the melted lead, after <term>projection</term> <note>The final stage of the alchemical process where the "Philosopher's Stone" is cast into molten base metal to transform it.</note>, showed the rarest and most beautiful colors imaginable while on the fire, finally settling into a green color. When it was poured out into an <term>ingot</term> <note>A block of metal, typically cast into a rectangular shape.</note>, it had the lively, fresh color of blood. Once cold, it shone like the purest and most splendid gold. Truly, all those standing around me were exceedingly startled. While this "aurified" <note>original: "aurified"; turned into gold.</note> lead was still hot, I ran with it to the goldsmith, who was astonished at its fineness. After a short trial by the <term>assay</term> <note>original: "test"; a procedure to determine the purity or value of a metal.</note>, he said it was the most excellent gold in the world.

The next day, news of this <term>prodigy</term> <note>An amazing or unusual thing, especially one seen as a miracle or omen.</note> spread throughout the Hague, so that many illustrious and learned people paid me friendly visits because of it. Among the rest was the general Assay-master and examiner of coins for the province of Holland, Mr. Porelius, who—along with others—earnestly begged me to put some of the gold through all their customary tests. I did so to satisfy my own curiosity. We went to Mr. Brectel, a silversmith, who first mixed four parts of silver with one part of the gold. He then filed the mixture down and added <term>nitric acid</term> <note>original Latin: "aquafortis", literally "strong water." It was used by alchemists and smiths to separate gold from silver.</note> to it. This dissolved the silver and allowed the gold to settle as a powder at the bottom. After the solution was poured off and the <term>residue</term> <note>original: "calx"; the powdery substance remaining after a metal has been burned or chemically dissolved.</note> of gold was washed with water, then refined and melted, it appeared as excellent gold. Instead of a loss in weight, we found the gold had actually increased; it had transmuted a <term>scruple</term> <note>A small unit of weight, roughly 1.3 grams.</note> of the silver into gold through its "abounding tincture" <note>In alchemy, the "tincture" is the powerful, transformative essence of the Philosopher's Stone that spreads through other metals.</note>.

Doubting whether the silver was now sufficiently separated from the gold, we mixed it with seven parts of <term>antimony</term> <note>A brittle, silvery-white semi-metal often used in the process of refining gold.</note>. We melted this and poured it into a cone, then blew off the <term>metallic residue</term> <note>original Latin: "regulus"; the pure metal that settles at the bottom of a crucible during smelting.</note> on a testing dish, where we missed eight...

### GUISEPPE FRANCESCO BORRI.

"The Rape of the Lock" and the graceful romance of "Undine" have familiarized everyone with the doctrine of elementary spirits <note>The belief in beings that inhabit the four elements: gnomes (earth), undines (water), sylphs (air), and salamanders (fire).</note>. However, the primary philosophical—or pseudo-philosophical—account of these unseen but not otherworldly intelligences is found in the small book by the Count of Gabalis, a series of conversations on the secret sciences. 

It is generally unknown that this work is little more than an uncredited translation of "The Key to the Cabinet of the Chevalier Borri, in which may be found various letters—curious, scientific, and chemical—with political instructions, matters that deserve the attention of the curious, and a variety of magnificent secrets."* Borri, who appears to have been a small-scale predecessor of Cagliostro <note>A famous 18th-century Italian occultist and adventurer.</note>, was born in Milan in 1627. In his early years, certain suspicious activities caused him to seek refuge in a church; however, later—much like Joseph Balsamo <note>The birth name of Count Cagliostro.</note>—he underwent a complete transformation and announced that he—

F. Borri, by means of which are seen various scientific, chemical, and very curious letters, with various political instructions, and other things worthy of curiosity and many very beautiful secrets.* Cologne (Geneva), 1681, 12mo.

The name of this spirit was <term>Homunculus</term> <note>original: "spook"; in alchemy, a homunculus is a miniature human being created through artificial means</note>, which, according to Paracelsus, signifies a minute human being generated unnaturally—without the assistance of the female organism—from the sperm of a man or a boy.

The monarch, determined to monopolize the talents of his adept, decided that the laboratory of <term>Borri</term> <note>Giuseppe Francesco Borri (1627–1695) was an Italian alchemist, prophet, and charlatan who traveled through European courts</note> should be transferred to his own palace. The alchemist, with an eye toward his freedom, objected that the power of his imp would be destroyed by any attempt to separate him from a certain vast iron furnace, which was the sulfurous abode of the Homunculus. However, his royal patron was a man of resources, and the furnace was also transported. Five years passed, and after Frederick III died, his successor determined to conduct a closer investigation into the transmutatory secrets of Borri. The alchemist took flight at the rumor of this inquiry but was arrested on the frontiers of Hungary and imprisoned at Vienna. There, he was claimed by the <term>Papal Nuncio</term> <note>a permanent diplomatic representative of the Pope</note> as a fugitive condemned for his heresies. He was sent to Rome and imprisoned in the Castle of Sant'Angelo. There, he was permitted to continue his alchemical processes, which were pursued unsuccessfully until his death in the year 1695.

"The Key to the Cabinet of the Chevalier Borri" has never been actually translated; the adaptation by the <term>Abbé de Villars</term> <note>author of 'Le Comte de Gabalis,' a famous 1670 satirical novel about Rosicrucianism and elemental spirits</note> is, of course, famous throughout Europe. As to the chemical secrets contained in the original letters, it may be safely concluded that they are few and unimportant.

# LASCARIS.

Bötticher lived in that town as a Prussian subject, but he was now a valuable prize. The Elector of Saxony <note>The Elector was the ruler of Saxony and one of the high-ranking princes who elected the Holy Roman Emperor.</note> made a competing claim for custody of him, and Bötticher decided to go to him. He was warmly welcomed, and after his transmutations <note>The supposed alchemical process of changing base metals into gold.</note> were witnessed, the title of baron was granted to him. He took up residence at Dresden, living in a style of great magnificence and wastefulness until every bit of his powder was used up. At that point, his extravagance left him deep in debt. 

His servants, whom he was unable to pay, spread the rumor that he intended to flee. The shortsighted <note>original: "purblind"</note> Elector refused to see that this sudden lack of money was proof that Bötticher was unable to create or increase the philosophers' stone <note>A legendary substance believed to turn base metals into gold and grant long life.</note>. Instead, the Elector surrounded Bötticher’s house with guards and held him essentially as a prisoner.

At this point, Lascaris, who was still traveling through Germany, took pity on the misfortunes of his young student <note>original: "neophyte"</note>. He tried to rescue him from his embarrassing situation with the help of a young doctor named Pasch, who was a personal friend of the now-noble apothecary’s apprentice. Their schemes resulted in the imprisonment of Pasch at the fortress of Sonneinstein, while Bötticher was closely confined in another castle at Kœnigstein.

Two and a half years passed. At the end of that time, Pasch succeeded in escaping, though he was badly injured <note>original: "at the expense of his limbs"</note> in the attempt. He died a few months later, bitterly complaining about the betrayal of the master alchemist <note>original: "adept"</note> Lascaris, who had completely abandoned him in his danger.

Bötticher remained in confinement with every opportunity to manufacture the philosopher's stone. However, he failed to do so. Nevertheless, between his training as an apothecary and his experiments in prison, he had become skilled in several branches of chemistry. He discovered the process for producing red porcelain, and afterwards—

Bötticher was restored to his favor and again enjoyed his title of baron, but once he had his liberty, he surrendered himself to an immoderately luxurious life and died in 1719 at the age of thirty-seven.

Bötticher was by no means the only apothecary’s apprentice <note>original: "boy"</note> who was enriched with the powder of Lascaris and sent out to preach the gospel of alchemy with practical demonstrations. Godwin, Hermann, Braun, and Martin of Fitzlar are mentioned among these half-initiated laborers, who shone until their stock-in-trade was exhausted and then disappeared one after another.

In the meantime, Lascaris himself was not idle. On February 16, 1609, he is believed to have changed mercury into gold and gold into silver. This was a double transmutation <note>The alchemical process of changing base metals into precious ones</note> considered by alchemical experts <note>original: "connoisseurs"</note> to be evidence of an unparalleled mastery <note>original: "adeptship"</note>. Liebkneck, a counselor of Wertherbourg, was a witness to this transmutation.

In the same year, a goldsmith of Leipzig <note>original: "Leipsic"</note> was visited by a mysterious stranger, who is unanimously identified as Lascaris. The stranger showed him an ingot <note>original: "lingot"</note> which he declared was manufactured by art, and which proved in assaying <note>The process of testing a metal to determine its purity</note> to be gold of twenty-two carats. It was purified by the goldsmith with antimony, and part of it was presented to him by the unknown man as a memorial of the alleged transmutation.

Shortly after, a lieutenant-colonel in the Polish army named Schmolz de Dierbach, who had inherited a belief in alchemical science from his father, was conversing on the subject at a café when he was approached by a stranger. This stranger presented him with some powder of projection <note>A substance alchemists believed could trigger the transformation of base metal into gold</note>. He then sailed with his wife for Malta. Do not regulate your actions according to the rules of common sense; instead, defy reason and courageously uphold every unbelievable absurdity. Remember that reproduction is the primary <note>original: palmary</note> active power in nature, politics, and society alike. It is a mania for mortals to desire to be immortal, to seek to know the future without understanding the present, and to strive to be spiritual while everything surrounding them is material.

After this speech <note>original: harangue</note>, the orator knelt devoutly <note>original: genuflected</note> before the deity of the temple and retired. At the same moment, a man of gigantic stature led the countess to the feet of the immortal Count de Saint-Germain <note>original spelling: Saint-German</note>, who spoke as follows:

"Chosen from my earliest youth for greatness, I dedicated myself to discovering the nature of true glory. Politics appeared to me as nothing but the science of deception. Military tactics seemed the art of assassination, and philosophy the ambitious foolishness of complete irrationality. Physics appeared as beautiful fantasies about Nature and the constant mistakes of people suddenly dropped into a country that is utterly unknown to them. Theology was the science of the misery that results from human pride, and history was the gloomy spectacle of unending betrayal and blundering. 

From this, I concluded that the statesman was a skilled liar, the hero an illustrious idiot, the philosopher an eccentric creature, the physician a pitiable and blind man, the theologian a fanatical teacher <note>original: pedagogue</note>, and the historian a mere dealer in words <note>original: word-monger</note>. Then I heard of the deity of this temple. I cast my cares upon him, along with my uncertainties <note>original: incertitudes</note> and aspirations. When he took possession of my soul, he allowed me to perceive all objects in a new light; I began to read the future <note>original: futurity</note>. This universe—previously so limited, so narrow, and so barren—was now expanded. I lived not only with those who exist now, but with those who lived in the past. He united me..."

When the service was finished, they changed back into their everyday clothes. A magnificent feast <note>original: repast</note> concluded the ceremony. During the course of the banquet, the two guests were informed that the <term>Elixir of Immortality</term> <note>A legendary substance believed to grant eternal life or youth.</note> was merely Tokay <note>A famous, expensive wine from the Tokaj region of Hungary.</note> colored green or red depending on the needs of the situation. They were told to spread the news with great mystery that the Count de Saint-Germain was five hundred years old, and that they must make gold—but above all, make victims to swindle.

The truth of this strange episode is not confirmed by any serious biographer. If it occurred as narrated, it certainly served to strengthen Cagliostro in his ambitious plans. The change which had taken place in the adventurer since his second visit to England is well described by Figuier <note>Louis Figuier (1819–1894), a French writer who documented the history of alchemy and pseudoscience.</note>.

At the same time, he talks little, more often wrapping himself in mysterious silence. <meta>This page continues the narrative of the occultist Alessandro Cagliostro's life following his departure from France.</meta>

-> ### LIVES OF ALCHEMICAL PHILOSOPHERS. <-

His discouraged followers learned that he was on the road to England.

Once in London, he recovered his energy. He was received with great honor; many of his disciples from Lyons and Paris followed him there. The English masons invited him to the metropolitan lodge and gave him the highest position, that of Grand Orient. He was urged to organize a masonic lodge of the Egyptian Rite and consented with some sadness, for the memory of the brilliant Paris lodge—which he had been on the verge of founding—was constantly on his mind. He could not comfort himself over the failure of that beautiful and long-cherished plan, which had cost him so much study, effort, and preaching.

It was from this safe distance that Cagliostro addressed his famous *Letter to the People of France*, which was translated into many languages and circulated widely throughout Europe. It predicted the French Revolution, the demolition of the Bastille, and the rise of a great prince who would abolish the infamous *lettres de cachet* <note>original: "lettres de cachet"; these were private warrants signed by the French King that allowed for the imprisonment of individuals without a trial or the right of appeal</note>, summon the States-General <note>The legislative assembly of the different classes of French subjects</note>, and re-establish the true religion.

The publication was extreme in its language and revolutionary in its sentiments. Shortly after its release, his well-known quarrel with the *Courrier de l’Europe* <note>original: "Courrier de l’Europe"; a French-language newspaper published in London that played a major role in exposing Cagliostro's past</note> began, which resulted in the exposure of Cagliostro's true life story from beginning to end.

Fearing the rage of his countless victims and extreme legal actions from his creditors, he hurried to leave London. He landed in Holland, crossed Germany, and took refuge in Basel, where the traditional hospitality of the Swiss cantons somewhat reassured the exposed occultist. From the moment of this exposure, however, Cagliostro's downfall was incredibly rapid. Nevertheless, he was still followed by some of his initiates, who...

--- Page 274 ---
<meta>This page continues the biographical account of Count Cagliostro, focusing on the circumstances of his imprisonment and conflicting descriptions of his physical appearance.</meta>

# LIVES OF ALCHEMISTICAL PHILOSOPHERS.

They were determined to release the entombed adept <note>Cagliostro, who was imprisoned by the Church</note>, but they were informed that Cagliostro was dead. “At this intelligence,” says Figuier <note>Louis Figuier (1819–1894), a French scientist and writer</note>, “they perceived plainly that the former *Parliament of France* was not to be compared with the Roman Inquisition; and without regretting the demolished Bastille, they could not but acknowledge that it disgorged its prey more easily than the Castle of Saint Angelo.”

The personal attractions of Cagliostro appear to have been exaggerated by some of his biographers. “His splendid stature and high bearing, increased by a dress of the most bizarre magnificence, and the extensive suite which invariably accompanied him in his wanderings, turned all eyes upon him and disposed the minds of the common people toward an almost idolatrous admiration.”

With this opinion of Figuier may be compared the counter-statement of the Italian biographer: “He was of a brown complexion, a bloated countenance, and a severe aspect; he was destitute of any of those graces so common in the world of gallantry, without knowledge and without abilities.” But the Italian biographer was a false witness, for Cagliostro was beyond all question and controversy a man of consummate ability, tact, and talent. The truth would appear to lie between these opposite extremes. “The Count de Cagliostro,” says the English biography published in 1787, “is below the middle stature, inclined to corpulency; his face is a round oval, his complexion and eyes dark, the latter uncommonly penetrating. In his address, we are not sensible of that indescribable grace which engages the affections before we consult the understanding. On the contrary, there is in his manner a self-importance which at first sight rather disgusts than allures, and obliges us to withhold our regards until, on a more intimate acquaintance, we yield it as a tribute to our reason.”

# CONCLUSION.

I have engaged in the serious study of alchemy and have found that the old theories are illustrated by new facts, while these new facts coincide with the old theories. As all of this has occurred—in the words of the alchemists—"by a natural process, devoid of haste or violence," I trust that it is no illusory discovery. I hope that its future publication may give a new impulse to the study of the <term>Hermetic writings</term> <note>Works attributed to Hermes Trismegistus, the legendary founder of alchemy.</note> among the <term>occultists</term> <note>Those who study hidden or supernatural arts and sciences.</note> of England and America.

<summary>The author concludes by asserting that modern evidence supports ancient alchemical theories and expresses hope that these findings will revitalize the study of Hermetic philosophy in the English-speaking world.</summary>

<keywords>alchemy, Hermetic writings, occultism, scientific synthesis, England, America</keywords>

<meta>This page begins an appendix providing historical context and supplementary details regarding the lives and reputations of specific alchemists and occultists mentioned in the preceding text.</meta>

# APPENDIX.

## I.

The life of Denis Zachaire has been made the subject of an interesting and well-written novel—*A Professor of Alchemy*—by "Percy Ross," recently published by Mr. George Redway. The life of the great adept, after his accomplishment of the **Great Work** <note>original: *Magnum Opus*; the successful creation of the Philosopher's Stone</note>, is detailed at some length, with M. Louis Figuier apparently being the authority for the basic facts of the case. The alchemist is described by the French writer as having traveled to Lausanne, where he fell in love with a young and beautiful lady. He allegedly took her from Switzerland into Germany and then abandoned himself completely to a life of wastefulness and folly, which ended tragically at Cologne in the year 1556. He was strangled in the middle of a drunken sleep by the cousin who had accompanied him on his travels, and who desired his wealth and his mistress. The murderer escaped with the lady, who appears to have been his accomplice. The sole authority for this story appears to be a poem by Mardoché de Delle, who served as a kind of poet laureate to the court of Rudolph II. It is quite likely a mere invention of the poet; there is nothing in the serious and restrained treatise written by Denis Zachaire during that period to support the account of his supposed extravagance.

## II.

The manuscript volume entitled "Egyptian Freemasonry" fell, along with the other papers of Cagliostro <note>Count Alessandro di Cagliostro (1743–1795), a famous Italian occultist and high-society magician</note>, into the hands of the Inquisition. It was solemnly condemned in their judgment as containing rites, propositions, a doctrine, and a system which opened a broad road to rebellion and were calculated to destroy the Christian religion. The book was characterized as superstitious, blasphemous, impious, and heretical. It was publicly burned by the hands of the executioner, along with the instruments belonging to the

<summary>This appendix discusses the fictionalized accounts of the alchemist Denis Zachaire's death and the Inquisition's condemnation and burning of Cagliostro's "Egyptian Freemasonry" manuscript.</summary>
<keywords>Denis Zachaire, Cagliostro, Egyptian Freemasonry, Inquisition, Magnum Opus, alchemy in literature, Rudolph II</keywords>

### A. E. WAITE
<note>Arthur Edward Waite (1857–1942) was a prolific British scholarly author on occult and esoteric matters.</note>
</insert>

<term>Alchemy</term>: An ancient branch of natural philosophy and an early form of chemistry, focused on the transmutation of matter, particularly the turning of base metals into gold and the discovery of a universal cure for disease.
<term>Mysticism</term>: The belief that union with or absorption into the Deity or the absolute, or the spiritual apprehension of knowledge inaccessible to the intellect, may be attained through contemplation and self-surrender.
<term>Alchemists</term>: Practitioners of alchemy who sought the Philosopher's Stone and the Elixir of Life.
<term>Arthur Edward Waite</term>: A prominent member of the Hermetic Order of the Golden Dawn and a key figure in the study of Western Hermeticism.

<meta>This page serves as a publisher's advertisement for a book on the Tarot by S. L. MacGregor Mathers, featuring a review from the Saturday Review and a partial label from another publication.</meta>

->Foolscap octavo <note>original: "Fcap. 8vo"; a book size approximately 6.75 by 4.25 inches</note>, cloth binding, price 5 shillings (including the pack of 78 cards).<-

## FORTUNE-TELLING CARDS.—THE TAROT: Its
## Occult Significance, Use in Fortune-Telling, and Method of Play, etc.
### By S. L. MACGREGOR MATHERS.

> "The designs of the twenty-one trump cards are extremely singular. In order to give some idea of the manner in which Mr. Mathers uses them in fortune-telling, it is necessary to mention them in detail, together with the general meaning which he attaches to each of them. The aspiring fortune-teller <note>original: "cartomancer"</note> may then draw his own particular conclusions, and he will find considerable room for interpreting them according to his own preferences. It should further be mentioned that each of the cards, when reversed, conveys a meaning opposite to its primary significance. 
>
> 1. **The Juggler** <note>original: "Bateleur"</note>, also called **Pagad**; the latter name is cited by Count de Gebelin as proof of the Oriental origin of Tarots, being derived from *PAG* (chief or master) and *GAD* (fortune). The Juggler symbolizes Will. 
> 2. **The High Priestess**, or female Pope, represents Science, Wisdom, or Knowledge. 
> 3. **The Empress** is the symbol of Action or Initiative. 
> 4. **The Emperor** represents Realization or Development. 
> 5. **The Hierophant** or Pope is the symbol of Mercy and Beneficence. 
> 6. **The Lovers** signify Wise Disposition and Trials surmounted. 
> 7. **The Chariot** represents Triumph and Victory over Obstacles. 
> 8. **Themis** or **Justice** symbolizes Equilibrium and Justice. 
> 9. **The Hermit** denotes Prudence. 
> 10. **The Wheel of Fortune** represents Fortune, whether good or bad. 
> 11. **Fortitude** symbolizes Power or Might. 
> 12. **The Hanged Man**—a man suspended head-downwards by one leg—means Devotion and Self-Sacrifice. 
> 13. **Death** signifies Transformation or Change. 
> 14. **Temperance** typifies Combination. 
> 15. **The Devil** is the image of Fate or Fatality. 
> 16. **The Lightning-struck Tower**, also called the House of God <note>original: "Maison-Dieu"</note>, shows Ruin and Disruption. 
> 17. **The Star** is the emblem of Hope. 
> 18. **The Moon** symbolizes Twilight, Deception, and Error. 
> 19. **The Sun** signifies Earthly Happiness. 
> 20. **The Last Judgment** means Renewal and the Determination of a matter. 
> 21. **The Universe** represents Completion and Reward. 
> 0. **The Foolish Man** signifies Expiation or Wavering. 
>
> Separate meanings, along with their respective opposites, are also attached to each of the other cards in the pack. Thus, when they have been dealt out and arranged in any of the combinations recommended by the author for the purposes of divination, the inquirer has only to use this little volume as a dictionary in order to read his fate."—*Saturday Review*.

<meta>This is the title page and introductory text for S. L. MacGregor Mathers' 1888 treatise on the Tarot.</meta>

<header>These cards are usually known by the name of Tarots. It is divided into</header>

# FORTUNE-TELLING CARDS — THE TAROT;

## Its Occult Significance, Use in Fortune-Telling, and
## Method of Play,
## BY S. L. MACGREGOR MATHERS.
## Author of "The Key of Solomon the King," <note>A famous 14th or 15th-century grimoire (book of magic) attributed to King Solomon, which Mathers translated and edited.</note> etc.

The pack of cards known by the name of the Tarot is composed of seventy-eight cards, which are divided into two groups: the first consisting of twenty-two cards, which are called the Major Arcana <note>original: "Arcana" — meaning "mysteries" or "secrets."</note>; and the second consisting of fifty-six cards, which are called the Minor Arcana. The Major Arcana are also called the trumps, and they are numbered from one to twenty-one, with the twenty-second card being the Zero, or the Fool. The Minor Arcana are divided into four suits, which are called Scepters <note>modern: Wands</note>, Cups, Swords, and Pentacles <note>modern: Coins or Disks</note>. Each suit contains ten pip cards <note>term: "pip cards" — the numbered cards from Ace to ten.</note>, from one to ten, and four court cards, which are called the King, the Queen, the Knight, and the Page.

---

->LONDON: GEORGE REDWAY, YORK STREET, COVENT GARDEN.<-
->1888.<-

<meta>Handwritten letter "R" at the bottom right corner of the folio.</meta>

<summary>This title page introduces S. L. MacGregor Mathers' influential 1888 work on the Tarot, defining the structure of the 78-card deck into Major and Minor Arcana. It establishes the basic terminology for the suits and court cards used in occult divination.</summary>
<keywords>Tarot, Major Arcana, Minor Arcana, S. L. MacGregor Mathers, Scepters, Cups, Swords, Pentacles, The Key of Solomon the King, George Redway, occultism, divination</keywords>
